mr president, i would like to thank you. the question of the role that cohesion policy can play in implementing energy policy is one to which my answer is very clear. it is that it can be of incredibly important importance, perhaps even in some regions of crucial importance. my examples come from northern sweden, where six years ago we formulated the regional structural fund programmes for this period, including that we would stimulate the development of sustainable energy solutions. it resulted in a complete explosion of small businesses and others working in environmental technology and energy conservation; it spread like wildfire, and a lot of new jobs were created in these small businesses. linking it with innovation work also resulted in the transfer of energy saving innovations in the forestry industry to the tourism industry. i am absolutely convinced that we can use cohesion policy to implement sustainable solutions and, moreover, to create jobs.

mr president, i would like to thank you. i am very positive about the agreement that is now on the table. it is another step towards a healthier and more credible credit rating culture. the main point is that the requirement for lenders to use external credit ratings will be removed from eu legislation by 2020. instead, lenders should take greater responsibility for making their own assessments. this is a crucial key to success. it is therefore very important that these changes in legislation are actually implemented. with lenders doing a broader analysis and taking more responsibility, credit ratings will start to play a more reasonable role. i also think that the new rules on the valuation of governments and government securities are well balanced: on the one hand, credit ratings must not be about political expediency but must be neutral and sound assessments of real creditworthiness. the downgrades of the institutions during the crisis have been overly marked by well-timed political developments. on the other hand, the regulatory framework should not be made more far-reaching than in this agreement. the existence of sound and independent valuations of sovereigns is a valuable factor in the credit market and must be maintained. thank you for your time.
madam president, i would like to thank you. thank you for your time. as i see it, the debate on stability bonds has recently developed in the right direction. collective borrowing is no longer seen as an emergency medicine, but rather as a promising alternative to effectively paying down debt and essentially stabilising the euro. this more long-term and structural oriented perspective is good. if any form of joint borrowing is to be successfully implemented, it must be done in a well-designed and controlled manner so that the new mechanisms really contribute to greater stability and sustainability. what the commission should now analyse further in the first instance is the idea of creating a 'redemption fund'. this is not only the most interesting model, but also a relatively new proposal which has not yet been sufficiently studied. in europe we have now built up a huge mountain of debt which must be dealt with as efficiently as possible. if we do not address this challenge rationally, there is a great risk that debt management will stifle growth and job creation for a long time. for the euro area countries, a temporary 'redemption fund' could be an important part of the solution to the problem. there is therefore every reason to continue actively to work on this issue.
vice-president of the commission. − madam president, the commission welcomes the interest of the european parliament in the state aid modernisation (sam) initiative and in the two commission proposals to amend the procedural regulation and the enabling regulation adopted on 5 december 2012. the commission appreciates the ongoing open dialogue with the european parliament on state aid modernisation. the input from parliament during the workshop on sam, organised by the committee on economic and monetary affairs in september 2012, and at the presentation of the commission’s 2013 work programme on competition to the economic affairs committee in october 2012 was valuable for the commission’s work. the two commission proposals for amended council regulations were sent to the european parliament, the council and the member states simultaneously after their adoption on 5 december 2012. prior consultations on the main orientations of the proposals were presented to parliament in advance in the same way as to member states. under article 109 of the treaty on the functioning of the european union, the council is responsible for consulting parliament on regulations that it adopts to apply articles 107 and 108 on state aid. the commission takes note of parliament’s wish to include a codecision procedure on these matters in potential treaty revision. however, this issue goes beyond the legal framework under which the two current proposals will be adopted. the commission is committed to keeping parliament informed on progress regarding the sam initiative and on public consultations and new guidelines adopted in that context. the commission looks forward to the appointment of the rapporteurs and to parliament’s opinion on both proposals. the commission services are available for the rapporteurs and other interested members to discuss the relevant issues and to provide any necessary clarification.
on behalf of the ppe group. – madam president, let me thank the rapporteur as well as the commissioner. i think that it is important to remember that state aid always risks distorting competition. it always risks conserving all structures at the cost of new entrants and new ideas and it is always a risk to the integration of the internal market. that is why we have competition legislation and that is why we have strict rules for state aid. i think it is important when we talk about the modernisation of state aid that the theme must be less but better targeted state aid. we have just had a discussion in this plenary about public finances and state aid always undermines the stability of public finances. that is why the target must be less and better targeted state aid, supporting knowledge development, supporting innovation clusters, supporting services – but not supporting different entities, different companies – supporting the emergence of new technologies in different areas. i think it must be up to the commission to follow up on our achieving less state aid – because then we are enabling more private investments and more competition – but also to ensure that it is better targeted and not distorting competition but helping new innovations and new science clusters and new infrastructures to emerge. that must be the challenge for the commission in the years to come.
mr president, the irish presidency takes office at a sensitive time for the union. i am thinking particularly of the financial framework negotiations. it is the first time in history that a long-term budget will be dealt with under the rules of the lisbon treaty and at the same time in an economic crisis, the deepest since the birth of this union. the crisis fosters a simple logic. if member states cut their budgets, so should the eu, but that logic is simplified and false. first of all, not all member states have cut their budgets and no member state has decided to cut their national budget up until 2020, so why should the european union? we should hopefully be out of the crisis by then. secondly, the main priority in this situation, when the role of the eu is more difficult and more important than ever, should be to safeguard the functioning, the unity and the future of the union, even if it comes at a cost. parliament has been quite clear about what we need in order to grant consent to the new financial framework. everyone should understand that the consent procedure gives parliament the right of veto. we do not need any major increase, but we do need a budget strong enough to match crucial priorities, investments in sustainability, growth and jobs, particularly youth unemployment and infrastructure, as well as to play a major role in the world. we need a budget that is flexible enough not just to meet present needs, but also the need for the future up to 2020, including a binding mid-term revision. we need a process towards more own resources stable enough to reduce the national direct contributions so controversial and detrimental to eu solidarity. i would like to ask the prime minister, even though mr van rompuy is now heading the negotiations, what he can do to deliver on those needs in order to avoid a veto from parliament?

in writing. − i abstained in the final vote. naturally it is good that the eu is trying to find ways to fight youth unemployment, but the issue of mass youth unemployment is not separate from the economic policy of the european union. the neo-liberal dogma dominating the eu commission and the council has led to severe austerity policies around europe. these policies are deepening the economic crisis, causing even more unemployment among young people. we need a new economic policy with ambitious government spending, to create millions of new green jobs. this is also the way to bring down the deficit. most importantly of all, we need to do it now. many years of very high unemployment do extreme damage to societies. there is also the risk that the youth guarantee is constructed in a way so that, as in sweden, it leads to bad working conditions, employers abusing the system and lower wages. a new economic policy can also have its own youth guarantee, which guarantees young people real rights to qualified, adequately paid and stable employment and social security.

in writing. - we abstain in the vote because we do not believe that a joint issue of government bonds (stability bonds) is the right way to solve the euro area debt crisis in the short term. while stability bonds can in the long run create greater stability in the euro area and lower borrowing costs for countries with financing problems, such a model risks at present creating the wrong incentives for both those member states managing their economies and those with high debt. the introduction of stability bonds also implies a very significant economic and institutional change with long-term consequences for the sovereignty of the member states. legal, constitutional and institutional issues must therefore be clarified before any introduction can be considered. a fundamental prerequisite for a common issuance of bonds in the euro area is also that these member states have significantly reduced their current deficit and debt levels. furthermore, there must be public acceptance of the major changes and possible risks that stability bonds would entail. we therefore want to stress the importance of a public debate on the pros and cons of introducing common bonds.

member of the commission. − mr president, i am replacing in this debate my colleague mr šemeta, who is on a round trip to a number of member states to prepare the ecofin council later on next week. the commission shares the concerns of the honourable members to reduce red tape and increase predictability for our traders. this is why, with the draft union customs code, we amend the current provisions of the code to make clear that eu non-preferential rules of origin apply only to imports. exports are not concerned here, so there will be no changes to the current practice. this is in line with wto commitments. we need to make sure that all member states apply one set of rules consistently. uniform implementation is a basic principle and an eu obligation, which i am sure the european parliament wants the commission and the member states to adhere to. on the import side, we have not yet made any proposal for implementing provisions pending the adoption of the union customs code. the commission confirms that the principle of last substantial transformation is enshrined in the wto agreement on rules of origin and in the eu customs code. this will continue to apply under the union customs code. currently, eu implementing provisions on the principle of last substantial transformation exist only for a limited number of products. some discussions have taken place in the customs code committee origin section to explore the possibility of establishing such provisions product by product, while taking account of the needs of smes. the commission considers that such clearer rules could make origin determination more predictable and ensure better application of the trade measures such as anti-dumping and other trade defence instruments and safeguard mechanisms. it should not be for the exporting country to determine the non-preferential origin of products. indeed, pending completion of the harmonisation work programme in the wto, importing countries are entitled to define the country of origin of goods imported into their territory, under strict conditions. pending agreement in the wto, it would not be in the interest of the eu to accept, a priori, whatever origin determination of imported goods is made by the authorities of the exporting countries. doing so would undermine the eu’s capacity to negotiate in the wto. in line with wto obligations, when an eu good is imported into a third country, the rules of origin of that third country – not of the exporting country – are the rules to be used to define its originating status in that third country. the requirements for accepting eu originating status of eu products exported to third countries are for those third countries to decide. any acceptance by third countries of non-preferential certificates of origin, issued for example by chambers of commerce in an eu member state, is purely voluntary and cannot be determined by eu regulation. the commission has no intention to introduce detailed rules for exports, so an image problem for european manufacturers does not arise, nor will there be any new need to assist operators. finally, i would like to convey again commissioner šemeta’s apologies for not being able to join us this evening. on the other hand, i believe it is a very fortunate moment to have this discussion, as we are actively preparing mc9 – the ministerial conference of the world trade organization – in december of this year, which will be discussing trade facilitation, and there uniform rules of origin are very important because that will in fact simplify the entry of goods into the european union and will be a contribution to the whole debate on trade facilitation.
for the ppe group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. sometimes the most important things are some of the most complicated, and have the most bizarre names and titles, such as customs regulations or rules of origin for goods not covered by preferential trade. i think this is just such a thing; something that is extremely important but also quite complicated. perhaps that is why, unfortunately, very few members are interested in it, and why it is so readily debated in the middle of the night in strasbourg. but this is very, very important. determining the origin of an item is very complicated. i think of the shirt i'm wearing, whose cotton comes from egypt, is woven in italy and sewn in hong kong. in the past, things were so much easier when a product was made in a country and was determined to belong to that country, to come from that country. but i believe that the most complex reality must be met by the simplest and clearest legislation. that is what has been troubling me about this particular issue. so far we have used the rule of last valuable processing to determine the country of origin, and i am pleased to hear the commissioner say that we will continue to use that rule. so far we have been clear that it is the exporting country that decides, but what we have seen is that the commission now wants to give the eu more power to decide which country is to be the country of origin, so that it is more the importing country that decides. i understand the ambition to make things more difficult and to avoid anti-dumping duties, but it is still a very small part of our trade - 0.7 per cent - that is actually covered by anti-dumping duties, so therefore the question is whether it is really worth it. i also hear the commissioner saying that these are to be simplifications and not changes, and i am glad you point that out. but i think the concern that our traders and importers have shown in this regard speaks for itself. i also know that a number of member states are concerned, not least because the proposal is partly based on a draft from the wto. draft laws are usually a poor basis for legislation. i think it would be better to wait until the wto negotiations on this issue are over before making any changes. i therefore hope that we can take the commissioner at his word that the essential final processing will continue to be decisive, and that we will base trade and rules of origin on the exporting country, and not build up our own bureaucracy to try to do the same. thank you, sir. answer "blue card". mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. yes, daniel caspary, i think i see the commissioner in much the same way. that's part of the problem, i think, the claim that this doesn't change anything, you can ask yourself why we're doing this. in particular, i think we should be careful about making changes that we do not think our trading partners would make in the same way. because if they did the same, maybe we'd react. if they were to make a different assessment of what should be the rules of origin than what we have decided here when we choose to export something. i think that much of this debate is really about the same problem that we are going to discuss tomorrow with regard to the 'made in' legislation on mandatory origin labelling. i hope the commission will also withdraw this.

writer. - madam president! - what is it? in a month's time, the un commission on women (csw) will hold its 57th session, which will seek to find common positions to combat violence against women more effectively. we in the committee on women's rights and gender equality have therefore put an oral question to the commission and a resolution to send a strong signal that the fight against violence against women and girls must be strengthened at global, local and eu level. our committee is also sending a delegation to csw to reinforce our message. i would like to emphasize that the motion for a resolution is the resolution of the whole committee; we have worked on it together and we want to make this a priority for our colleagues in parliament. i would also like to pay special tribute to my predecessor as president, eva-britt svensson, who worked throughout her time in the european parliament to put these issues at the top of the agenda. she succeeded, and we're continuing her work. let me tell you briefly why it is so crucial to prevent and stop men's violence against women. not a day goes by that we don't read about women who have been beaten to death, women who have been exploited in prostitution, women who have been raped by someone they know and trust. girls and women are threatened with male violence every day of their lives; it is part of their upbringing. this is nothing less than a systematic violation of their human rights. most women set up their lives around the threat of male violence against women. they take different roads when they go home at night, they avoid public transport at certain times of the day, they make sure to go in groups if they are going out to have fun and dance. men's threats of violence and aggression, even when they don't lead to it, are also a form of violence. it diminishes and restricts a woman's freedom of movement. it sets limits on her behavior and establishes an unequal power structure where men are in charge, and women are to set themselves up. violence by men against women is a violation of human rights. it is also one of the biggest obstacles to gender equality. we also need to take this opportunity to shift the focus for a moment to shine a light on those who commit these crimes, those who threaten, beat and murder: the men. many people find it unpleasant to talk about the perpetrators. most people want to emphasize that there are actually quite a few men who do beat up. some would like to suggest that the ones who strike are some special categories of men, social, economic, or cultural. but it's not like that. no, the problem with male violence against women is that it is built into the structure of power in our societies. the men who beat come from all different social classes, all different backgrounds. the only thing they have in common is that they're men. and so the conclusion has to be that we have a common problem in how we shape masculinity in our societies. but, yes, the percentage of men who are beaten and threatened is relatively low. one problem, however, is that very few men are actually actively engaged in working against male violence against women. even those who do not beat or threaten do not often take a clear stand against men's violence against women. our committee has repeatedly raised these issues and pointed out how the eu should work. for example, we have said many times that we want the commission to come forward with proposals for an eu strategy but also a minimum directive on violence against women. of course, we are still waiting for that. we now present an oral question and a resolution setting out some important actions that the eu should take globally. the resolution sets out a number of different aspects and measures, and i will only touch on a few here: the eu delegation has a key role to play in the csw negotiations. the eu delegation must stand up for women's human rights throughout the negotiations. the eu also has a very important role to play in ensuring that there is concrete action and commitment. it is about, for example, information campaigns, it is about an international implementation plan, it is about getting increased resources, and it is about being reflected in eu aid policy and in eu foreign policy. men's violence against women is not a must, it is not something that is predestined. women and girls have the right to live a life without fear, to feel safe in their own bodies, in their own homes, in their own schools, in their own workplaces, in their own public spaces. we as politicians have a responsibility to listen to all the women's organisations and other actors who work on these issues on a daily basis and to take their recommendations to translate them into political action. together we can put an end to violence against women and girls in the eu and globally. in writing. older people can have a better life with the help of technological progress and innovation. many older people may not discover communication technologies and the internet until after retirement. the ease with which people can stay in touch with friends, family and carers has great potential to enrich europe's older people. digital technologies have other benefits. in the final stages of life, when the need for care becomes especially great, such inventions as safety alarms with built-in video and voice functions can help relatives and acquaintances to quickly get in touch with the elderly. however, such innovations require that we have a neutral platform to launch them on. we need net neutrality and an open internet without unnecessary commercial blocks that hinder the launch of new technological tools. that is why we as a parliament need to keep a close eye on what commissioner neelie kroes will write for recommendations to telecom operators. we have strong indications that the commissioner-in-office is not able to preserve the platforms we need to ensure that everyone, young and old, has a dignified and constructive presence in the news.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on development. − mr president, tomorrow this parliament has the chance to end over-fishing, not only in europe but also in other parts of the world. we are now importing 65% of all the fish that we consume; we are the world’s largest fish market. that is clearly not sustainable. the commissioner has made it clear that if the eu does not radically reform its common fisheries policy now, only eight out of all the commercially exploited fish stocks will be at sustainable levels in 2022. that would mean importing more and fishing more elsewhere, and just going on destroying what is left of our once-abundant seas. tomorrow we will have the responsibility and the opportunity to change this destructive route. the rodust report, as adopted in the committee on fisheries, was a real step towards ending over-fishing. we want to impose the obligation to rebuild fish stocks, and not just to minimum levels where they possibly could produce a steady yield; we want to rebuild them to a level exceeding this. norway and russia have proved that this is possible. in the barents sea, if they had aimed to keep the cod stock at maximum sustainable yield (msy) levels they would now only have half a million tonnes of spawning stock biomass there. but they have aimed to rebuild the stock above msy levels, and they now have two million tonnes – four times msy levels. this proves that it is not only environmentally sound to rebuild fish stocks above minimum msy levels, but it also means good business. fish a little less now and spend a lot less money trying, and earn a lot more money in the future. that is the good future we want to see for the fishermen of europe. if we aim only at msy levels we are condemning fishermen to continue to struggle where stocks are balancing at the point of msy and could at any time be deemed over-fished, which would mean that limits would have to be imposed on fishing. fishermen do not want that; they want stability in catches, they want safe investments and they want to be able to tell their banks that they are going to have a future because stocks are so abundant that there are no risks at all involved in their fisheries. i am very happy that the majority of our fisheries committee supported this view, and i hope to see even stronger support from parliament as a whole. we must also recall the position of the council, which i find quite shocking. they, too, commit to rebuilding fish stocks in their approach, but have made a little addition: the two words ‘where possible’, without defining ‘where possible’. i think it is very important that citizens of europe are made aware of this. member states have not thought it ‘possible’ to set quotas at sustainable levels over the last 30 years, so why would they now think it is possible? the eu should show leadership on sustainable fisheries to the rest of the world. i want to thank ms rodust and all our colleagues because we are on the way to achieving that together. i agree with mr coveney that we need a modernisation of fisheries policy.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. there is a lot to be said about fisheries policy, but i can only make two brief comments. first of all: we have to stop the waste of completely edible fish being thrown overboard. we need a ban on this to ensure sustainable fishing. all catches must be landed. nothing is to be thrown back into the sea. secondly, as regards the maximum sustainable yield (msy): today, two out of three fish species are overfished. to be above msy is to be above this problem. it is to think about the future, to put oneself on the safe side rather than risking the disappearance of fish stocks. if we apply stricter rules for a number of years now, by 2020 the fishing industry could have increased catches, increased fishermen's wages and created thousands of new jobs. to achieve sustainable fisheries that respect our ecosystems, we need to take important steps today. the proposals of the committee on fisheries are good and could certainly be strengthened further. but i ask everyone to put aside short-term interests and instead look at the big picture. it will pay off in the future with more fish, with bigger fish, many more jobs in the fishing industry and strengthened ecosystems.
mr president, i would like to thank you. we are at a point where we can make a historic decision, and my heroes in this context are the commissioner and our rapporteur, ulrike rodust. i'm glad we can put an end to the predatory fishing and have a sustainable policy. i want to mention two things that are also important. one is fish farming, which previous speakers were in on. it is a growing market in northern europe and we must ensure that it is done in a sustainable way. it creates jobs, but it has to be done in an environmentally sustainable way, and there we have a job to do to say what is sustainable and what is not. the second is sport/recreational fishing, which is so incredibly important to the growing tourist industry. we must not destroy what we have in sweden, which is very good, namely local fisheries management areas where all stakeholders are involved and take responsibility and own the issue of sustainable fishing in their area. this is of great importance for sport fishing and i want us to be vigilant about it and it must not be broken up by eu directives.
in writing. the european parliament voted today on two reports on agriculture in the outermost regions, which include french, portuguese and spanish colonies such as guadeloupe, the azores and the canary islands, and the greek islands in the aegean sea. the islands receive most of their income from tourism. production is not primarily agricultural, nor is income derived from it. the conditions for farming are not ideal. transporting products from french guiana in south america and from the azores in the middle of the atlantic to a european market is neither market nor environmentally sustainable. the report argues that for these reasons the eu should provide additional production, export and import support. i believe that a major reform of the agricultural policy would be preferable, making it easier for eu citizens to trade with non-eu countries too. the reports also contain other problematic writings, relating to the "impact of immigration on the situation on the islands" and calling for greater investment in coupled support. the nature of the market economy is to change markets and to reorient production towards new goods and new customers. these islands already have a thriving tourist industry to develop further. making people dependent on aid and subsidies does not help them or their descendants. that is why i voted against the report.

in writing. - i voted against the report. the reason is that my party opposes the eu's common agricultural policy. this is because we are anti-federalists and believe that agricultural policy should be re-nationalised. agricultural policies also lead to a huge waste of resources, contribute to environmental degradation and to the poor export prospects of agricultural products from many countries in the global south. the majority of the outermost regions of the eu belong to france for historical reasons. they were former french colonies that were later declared departments of the french state. we believe that france has its own historic responsibility to lift these regions out of poverty and vulnerability.

in writing. − harmful noise levels are one of the most widespread health problems in the eu and we swedish social democrats want to see measures to reduce the negative impact of noise on people. reduced noise levels are also important for future housing construction. we therefore support lower limits for motor vehicle noise levels, a line also supported by the swedish government. we would point out, however, that we would have liked to see longer lead times for the introduction of these limit values, in order to give the automotive industry a better chance of adapting. this is something we will continue to work on in negotiations with the council. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work. this means that the regulation and the limit values may be amended if the commission deems it necessary, for example, in the light of technical conditions.
in writing. - reduced noise levels from motor vehicles are important, and clear noise labels for new cars would bring major health benefits. we supported the ambitious proposal of the european parliament's committee on the environment, and although important parts of the proposal were not supported, we voted in favour of the report. the final report is not what we wanted, but it is a basis on which to build.

deputising for the rapporteur. − mr president, on behalf of mr casa who regrets that he cannot be here, i will present some of the views that he has been eager to get through in this discussion. first of all, it goes without saying that, in a time of fiscal consolidation and necessary budget efforts, it is also necessary to implement measures that can minimise all the losses to the eu as a whole but also to the individual member states. we cannot accept fraud under any circumstances, and that is why the commission put forward a proposal which is a useful quick fix in tackling vat fraud, which is a problem that is costing all our member states and the eu as a whole billions of euros every year. for example carousel fraud alone cost the eu in the region of eur 14 billion between 2005 and 2006 and, in addition to that, we also get the negative effects regarding those who are dealing with businesses in a legitimate and legal way; it is unfair competition in that sense. the measures that we have tried to combat vat fraud have historically been very much aimed at those who are doing it right – which is a problem because they are doing it right. just changing the legal structures is not enough. you need also to be able to fight those who are ignoring the rules. that is why it is necessary to change the approach to combating vat fraud. all measures which are legitimate will be accepted, of course, but we need to ensure that everyone complies with them, and we need to ensure that those who do not comply with the rules are forced to. mr casa’s report therefore supports the commission’s proposal aimed at preventing further losses through vat fraud, and this proposal would enable member states to deal efficiently with vat fraud through a quick-reaction mechanism. this proposal is small and unambitious but a realistic step in fighting vat fraud. however, to properly combat vat fraud, more needs to be done such as structurally reforming the vat system. parliament is currently preparing an own-initiative report aimed at fighting tax fraud in a more general sense. not only will it support the current commission proposal, it will go beyond in coupling the proposals with long-term strategies to combat tax fraud. it is therefore necessary to restate the message of mr casa’s report in a green paper on vat. a simplified vat system across the eu will assist in reducing the complexity which exists between different member states. it will create a system which is more robust and therefore less susceptible to vat fraud but, i would like to add, also making it easier for entrepreneurs all over europe to deal with business cross-border because that is one of the problems we encounter today when deepening the internal market. despite the benefits of this proposal there is also discussion of whether this means we are entering a new phase of european taxation. i think it is important to say that this is not the case. this is a way of getting better reaction and better opportunities to deal with vat fraud by using the different opportunities in different member states. member states are therefore urged to cooperate and follow this and see the opportunities because this is an advantage for all of us. the more we can fight tax fraud in a simple way, the more we can ensure that honest entrepreneurs and businesses can deal with cross-border business, the better it is. of course it is important to have a brief consultation period prior to the implementation of any anti-fraud measures but the commission’s proposal is a first logical step in combating vat fraud and we welcome that. i appreciate that the commissioner is here and i appreciate that those who have been involved in this dossier are also here to follow up the discussion.
the s&d group is in favour of the proposal. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. it seems that there is now broad support in parliament for the establishment of a rapid mechanism against vat fraud. i think this is a very positive signal. it is excellent that parliament is leading the way on these kinds of issues. in the fight against tax fraud, there is much to be gained from improving the efficiency and scope of the eu's work. in particular, given that a large proportion of tax fraud today is cross-border, strong eu mechanisms are necessary to be able to put in place the necessary countermeasures. as much as €1 trillion a year is lost in the eu through tax fraud and tax evasion. if we could, with the help of intensified eu cooperation in this area, start to push that figure downwards, it would be of great importance for the stability and sustainability of public finances. the debate in the committee on economic and monetary affairs on the commission's proposal for a rapid mechanism against vat fraud has been very constructive. we have agreed on several important reinforcements to the proposal: first, the commission should continuously work to extend the mechanism with additional anti-fraud tools. reverse charge is usually a useful tool, but should be complemented by several. secondly, the transparency of the process should be increased. if all member states and other relevant actors are kept fully informed about what is happening with an application, cooperation will flow better. thirdly, it is essential that the commission has sufficient administrative and human resources to enable the mechanism to operate as quickly and smoothly as it is intended. fourthly, the commission should report regularly on the functioning of the mechanism. fifthly, the commission should also, in addition to the fast-track mechanism, take measures to speed up the ordinary procedure for member states wishing to take specific measures against vat fraud. an objective should be to conclude such a procedure within five months. i hope that this strong signal from parliament can now influence the process in the council of ministers in a positive way. unfortunately, several member states seem to want to delay or even stop the fast track mechanism. that's not good enough! the rapid mechanism is a simple and reasonable improvement to the regulatory framework which should be easy to accept. if we are to seriously tackle tax fraud, this is one of the many steps we must be prepared to take. thank you for the floor, and a big thank you to the rapporteur.
for the alde group. thank you, mr president, commissioner, and also a thank you to mr casa, and i will let gunnar hökmark speak. a common vat system is necessary to promote crossborder trade, it has been pointed out. the eu's vision for a future vat system should be to create a simpler, more efficient and more robust system. it shall be transparent and reduce the administrative burden on businesses. at the same time, such a system must be fraud-proof. today, as has been said here, vast sums of money from the legal economy are going straight into the pockets of the criminals. as gunnar hökmark has indicated, the difficult budgetary negotiations which are still ongoing at the moment show that in the future we will have fewer resources to implement the policies which are essential for creating jobs and growth. we need to protect our resources better. the commissioner has mentioned the carousel fraud, as well as the amount of money that is missing. we have also seen how emissions trading can make billions disappear in just a few moments. the commission and algirdas šemeta have put forward a number of good initiatives to access tax havens and comprehensive tax planning. another good example is eurofishing, an internet tool for the rapid exchange of information between member states. and now we must give the member states better and more effective tools to combat vat fraud as well. vat fraud consists of cross-border criminal networks that exploit the legal and administrative loopholes in the eu vat system in a rapid, coordinated and flexible manner. open borders are good, but as we know, they are also exploited by criminals. as recently as last year, several people were convicted of pretending to sell millions of mobile phones across europe through a brussels logistics company the network managed to steal €203 million before they could be stopped. the commission now proposes that when member states discover widespread and coordinated vat fraud, they should be able to immediately derogate from the vat rules, so that the so-called reverse taxation obligation will be able to stop criminal networks more quickly and effectively. this is good, but we must also bear in mind that this may create administrative burdens and problems for those companies which are honest and want to do the right thing, so we need a balanced solution. the national tax authorities need to improve their risk assessment, where europol has a key role to play in identifying criminal networks and coordinating information between member states. there have been many swedes in this debate, member of the commission. − mr president, i would like to thank the members for the views they have expressed during the debate. as you may know, the irish presidency sees this matter as a major priority. however, the positions of member states are still very divergent, and even opposing, on the definition and the application of unanimity in fiscal matters. sometimes it is very difficult to understand when member states which advocate stepping up the fight against tax fraud and tax evasion oppose a concrete proposal from the commission which could be of significant benefit in the fight against vat fraud. your strong support and constructive approach in this area is a very important signal for the member states. as i said earlier, the commission can accept some of the amendments, in particular on the reporting obligations concerning the functioning of the quick reaction mechanism. it will endeavour, as far as possible, to take these amendments into consideration during deliberations in the council. many of you spoke about the broader context of the fight against tax fraud and evasion. i would say to you that we have to look at this initiative as one of the initiatives which are now being presented both to the council and to the parliament. i would remind you that in 2011 the commission adopted the vat strategy. many of its initiatives either have already been proposed or will be proposed soon, such as initiatives for simplification. many of you talked about the need to simplify the vat system. very soon we will put forward a proposal on a standard vat declaration, which will significantly simplify other activities for administrative work and for businesses. for your information, on 1 january 2013 the new system of rules came into force. it has meant that e-invoicing is now considered to be just as valid as paper invoicing; a very important simplification measure. it has also allowed the introduction of cash accounting for small businesses. this means that, if the member states decide to introduce it into their national legislation, small businesses which face problems with cash flow will be able to use this mechanism. issues concerning vat fraud are of course also tackled in the vat strategy. we are also expanding the activity of eurofisc, with a much more efficient exchange of information within the framework of eurofisc and automated access to information. new initiatives will come over the course of this year and next year, and the strategy will be also implemented with the proposal on a definitive vat system. we have to remember that for very many years – 19, i think– we have lived with a transitional vat system. our intention is to present a proposal which will move us to a definitive vat system. the same could be said about the general issue of the fight against tax fraud and tax evasion. as i said in my introduction, in december 2012 we adopted an action plan to fight against tax fraud and evasion. there are 34 measures in that action plan, including the quick reaction mechanism. i look forward to the discussions in this parliament on the kleva report and hope that this will also facilitate our fight against tax fraud and evasion. i would like once again to thank the honourable members for their strong support for this initiative.
rapporteur. − mr president, first of all i regret that mr bloom has left the chamber because i wanted to thank him for his honesty in confessing that he feels he is not as smart as the criminals. i do not know to whom he is comparing himself so i cannot judge if he is right or wrong, but i think we can all admire his open mind in raising that issue here in the plenary. but i would also like, on behalf of mr casa, to thank the commissioner and our colleagues for their comments and cooperation regarding this report, and i will certainly forward to him the kind words that have been said in this debate. i think that even if we will all miss him in this chamber, i am sure that we all wish him good luck in the election campaign in malta. i think it is important to underline one thing, and that is that the fight against fraud and the fight against those committing fraud is a fight for the honest citizen and a fight for fair competition and good opportunities for entrepreneurship. because if we have rules, but some people do not apply these rules, then this makes the situation of those people who are following these rules much more complex. so there is a requirement on all legislators not only to legislate, but also to ensure that the legislation is followed and accepted and implemented, because that is how the rule of law should function. i think that when we talk about carousel fraud this is also a way of underlining the need not only to clarify that the rules should be respected, but also to ensure that we can simplify them. i would like to send that as a one-word message to you, commissioner, to guide your future steps: simplicity. simplicity is the way to defend the honest entrepreneur, the smes and all the other companies who are doing a fair and good job and to hinder the people who are trying to misuse and abuse the rules unlawfully. so simplicity and ensuring that we have fair competition should be the main theme.

in writing. − i voted in favour of the new legislation on alternative dispute resolution and online dispute resolution, which will enhance the possibilities for consumers and businesses to solve claims outside of court through quicker, easier and cheaper procedures. i welcome that this proposal does not reinvent the wheel, but rather builds on existing dispute resolution mechanisms in the member states, while encouraging those countries which do not have one to put one in place. it is also important to raise awareness about these mechanisms and make them known across borders. i therefore support that companies are encouraged to inform customers about which adr they operate under. in addition, i welcome the creation of an online platform, in which standard forms and electronic translation will further facilitate matters for business and consumers. these rules are about making the internal market work more effectively in practice, without tampering with legal certainty.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i voted in favour of the report on social entrepreneurship and it is encouraging that both the commission and parliament are paying attention to citizens who work together, build networks of friendship and improve their coexistence. i would like to stress again, however, that we have a very big problem in our entrepreneurship and our intellectual property rights. copyright commercializes all private interactions in a way that does not support either small-scale or social entrepreneurship. everything from shared cultural beliefs to the layout of text messages or user interfaces on websites is being brought into a largely monopolistic market regime. the blindness to patent infringement affects web developers, artists and small business owners, and we largely ignore that problem when we legislate. so i think it is of the utmost importance that we take this entrepreneurship seriously and maybe in the future also take the bull by the horns and actually create a communication model that encourages entrepreneurship and also small business owners.
in writing. − instead of highlighting the important issues of gender equality that can be solved, a number of other issues are highlighted, such as alternative economic models, government budgetary savings and so on. we regret that the report focused on issues other than gender equality and that we were unable to support it.
in writing. - i voted for this report as a whole because it is an important and good report which both describes the problems of gender equality policy with pervasive gender stereotypes and makes important recommendations on how we should act against them. however, for the sake of clarity, i voted against the whole of paragraph 17 (which refers to a 1997 european parliament resolution, including the prohibition of pornography) and also against paragraphs 50 and 51 (eu legislation on gender quotas for company boards).
in writing. - the resolution tabled by the committee on equality before parliament weighed heavily on me before this plenary session. now, our full house has voted to reject industry self-regulation, and a direct reference to a total ban on porn in all media. however, it is extremely unfortunate that we still refer to the 1997 resolution which seeks to combat all pornography in all media. pornography can rightly be considered a very large field of expression, some of which promote a less heavy-handed and conservative view of people's normal interactions. i voted against the resolution because i do not believe that neither we women nor society as a whole are harmed by the realization that sexuality exists and can be portrayed in film or on screen.
in writing. the report goes a long way in the question of how the eu should govern the daily lives and freedoms of its citizens. we consider that issues such as the labour market, education and the media are national issues and therefore cannot support the report.
madam president, mr cioloş, i would like to thank you for your kind words. i have been the alde group's shadow rapporteur on the rural development programme and i have to thank luis manuel capoulas santos and all my colleagues in the shadow rapporteur group for a fantastically nice and creative job. it is extremely important that we look at the whole of rural development. it is so obvious that agriculture and forestry are the foundation of the european countryside, wherever we are. but we must also realise that there can be no farmers, especially in the future, unless we have a socially and culturally functioning rural society. as regards the details of all these programmes, i must say that there are some details which i am particularly pleased about, and these are local logistics chains - particularly in the more vulnerable villages - the animal welfare is clearly on the rise, the ecological corridors now seem to be becoming a reality. because the most recent research shows that wild insects are four to six times as important for pollination and crops as domesticated bees. but i - and the whole alde group - have one objection, and that is against market regulation. i am on my knees that we must vote out all possibilities for export subsidies. we cannot pass our problems on to the poorest people on earth.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the committee on agriculture's proposals are making clear progress on certain points - mainly, i would say, thanks to our excellent rapporteur, mr luis manuel capoulas santos. thanks a lot to luis. however, on some key points, the majority voted against a progressive line of reform. that is why we social democrats have tabled amendments to restore the erosion of environmental rules, the so-called cross-compliance: the compulsory introduction of a greener use of direct payments. to remove the astonishing idea that a farmer can be paid twice for the same thing. to set a ceiling on the amount of support each landowner can receive. that citizens know who receives eu aid and that export subsidies that harm the agriculture of poorer countries are eliminated. all these proposals were voted down by the epp group of the moderates and the christian democrats. so my question to swedish moderates and christian democrats is: where are you going to change the position of your group and support a progressive reform of agricultural policy?
you know, ask for a blue card. i would like to ask my good friend othmar karas a very simple question. we agreed in the constitutional committee on the principle that each country should have representatives in the parliament according to the number of its citizens. that is the motion in this house. austria, represented inter alia by othmar karas, now wants this rule not to apply to austria. it applies to the netherlands, romania, france - it applies to all other countries, but austria, in the form of its representative here, wants the principle not to apply to austria. i'm going to ask you a very simple question. tell the whole house here why the difference of more than a million citizens should not affect the allocation of seats to austria? austria has a million fewer citizens than sweden, but you want the same number of seats as sweden. explain why austria, of all countries, should have a better representation in the parliament.
mr president, i would like to thank you. we agreed in the constitutional committee on the principle that member states are given seats in parliament according to their population, but that the smaller you are, the more members you have per voter. that's the result now. it's a principle, it's a rule. i asked my colleague just now how it is possible to explain that a difference of more than a million citizens would mean that you would still have the same number of seats as the larger country, and i should like to emphasize, mr president, that i did not receive an answer to that question. no answer to that question. the only answer i got to that question was that it was wrong from the beginning, so it's gonna be wrong in the end. it was wrong from the beginning, and therefore it must be wrong in the end. mr. speaker, i believe we are all united here by a desire to do what is right. if something is wrong, you make it right, and the constitutional committee did the right thing. now many austrian members want to do wrong again, but they have no answer to the question: why should austrian voters have more meps than any other country? why should austria deviate from the principle we have agreed? mr president, i did not get an answer to that question, and the reason is very simple: the only argument in favour of the amendment from austria which would break the proposal we have agreed in committee is that it was wrong from the start and therefore it must be wrong at the end. but i would say to the house that there can never be a rule for decision-making in this house or anywhere else but that we should keep the error without doing the right thing and therefore we should vote for what the committee proposes.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it is reasonable and logical that small eu countries with roughly the same population should have equal representation. unfortunately, the battle has ended up between sweden and austria, and the debate forgets that sweden has a million more citizens than austria. this means that it will be a just and democratic solution for sweden to retain its 20 seats while austria loses one. in this way, the members of both countries will represent approximately 475 000 citizens per member. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environment from harmful substances. therefore, i recommend that we follow the committee's line in the vote tomorrow. anything else would be a big failure.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the original proposal for the redistribution of seats in the european parliament has one deviation. a sinkhole in that otherwise fine, smooth curve, and that's sweden. that is why we are, of course, focusing so much on the swedish example in this debate. sweden would lose one of our 20 members of parliament and we would therefore also have to pay considerably more for each mandate than, for example, austria or hungary. we have argued against this obvious injustice, and not only on the swedish side. we've had support from a lot of other people. i'm grateful for that. the constitutional committee has also heard this. i'm very happy about that, too. i now ask parliament to support the committee's new proposals and to reject amendment no 8 in all its strength. it is not a matter of speaking for oneself, it is not a matter of talking about constitutional technology. it is about doing justice, creating justice and acting democratically. i hope that all these good elements of the debate will indeed be reflected in the vote tomorrow.
the rapporteur. - i would like to begin by thanking all the shadow rapporteurs for their good cooperation in the preparation of this report. i hope that in the vote tomorrow we will have a large majority in favour of the compromises we have made together. i would also like to thank all the others who have given valuable support and valuable comments during the course of the work. we are exposed to a number of hormone disrupting substances on a daily basis. they're in food packaging, skin creams, and in the dust on the floor. the available research indicates that even in very small amounts, endocrine disrupting chemicals contribute to a variety of hormone-related disorders. it can lead to breast and prostate cancers, impaired sperm quality, and birth defects, among other things. recent studies also show a link between endocrine disruptors and, for example, adhd, diabetes, and obesity. young children and fetuses are especially sensitive, and what they are exposed to too early in life can have consequences far later, in some cases even for the next generation. a more stringent regulation of endocrine disruptors could therefore save us a great deal of human suffering and society a great deal of money. in the report we call for better and more ambitious legislation to protect human health from endocrine disruptors. it is just as, for example, the eu environment agency has emphasised high time to act now. the scientific status reports commissioned by the european commission and unep/who respectively underline this fact. the report also calls for the introduction of information and testing requirements to identify chemicals that are endocrine disruptors, the development of common criteria for what is to be defined as endocrine disrupting substances, and that these criteria are scientifically based and that no single criterion such as potency alone should be the deciding factor, that endocrine disrupting chemicals should be considered as particularly dangerous substances under reach and other relevant chemical legislation; that endocrine disrupting substances should therefore be banned and replaced by less dangerous alternatives, or that their use be restricted, that the criteria for what is an endocrine disrupting substance take into account possible combined effects and the fact that people under development, for example sometimes foetuses, are extra sensitive, that substances with a structure similar to endocrine disrupting substances should also be assessed as endocrine disrupting substances if the manufacturer cannot preserve the endocrine disruptors, that the risk of endocrine disrupting substances should not be considered as being lower than the risk of determining, for example, which substances should be considered endocrine disrupting substances at lower doses. the last two points have been the subject of much debate in the last few days. the unep/who report on the state of research clearly explains why it is impossible to set safe limit values for endocrine disruptors. nevertheless, in this report we have made a compromise and left the door open to acceptance if industry can show that safe limits exist. i therefore sincerely hope that we can support the compromise that paragraph 25 actually is. the possibility of grouping substances as mentioned in paragraph 17 does not change the fact that substances that the manufacturer can prove are safe, despite similarities with substances judged to be endocrine disruptors, should in fact be allowed. so i hope that everyone can support paragraph 17 tomorrow. we naturally expect the commission to take our demands seriously and to put forward the legislation we are asking for as soon as possible.
the rapporteur. - mr president! - what is it? thank you very much, commissioner potočnik. i think you said what i was going to say about thresholds. i have made a great effort - and i know that all my colleagues have also made an effort - to follow the current state of research and the scientific state of affairs. now, the who/unep study did not exist when we compromised until the wording of paragraph 25, but i think it still very much reflects what is written in the who report, namely that it is likely that there can be no thresholds. we must therefore assume that it is impossible to define them, but we are not closing the door to industry to show that there are safe thresholds and that research can develop in a way that allows us to establish safe thresholds. if one is keen to follow the scientific evidence or facts that are available, one can very well support paragraph 25 and one can certainly not think that it goes too far. i would like to emphasize something that commissioner potočnik also said at the beginning, namely that people expect someone to be in control of all these subjects that we have around us. they expect us in the european parliament, in the european commission and in the governments of the member states to actually protect them from dangerous substances. today, many people are concerned, and rightly so, because they also realise that the scientific situation is the same and that legislation is lagging behind. that's why it's so important that we actually catch up and do what we probably should have done a long time ago. i also do not share the fear that some may have that this will cause very big problems for the chemical industry, which is a very innovative industry and which, like other industries, is also happy that we are making demands because it actually also promotes innovation and competitiveness. in addition, there are many companies that are asking for the possibility to buy safer products. the companies that sell clothes, build our houses or sell bottles of nappies or whatever it is want to be able to trust that the chemicals they use are actually safe so that they don't have to face the shame of having sold a shirt or a piece of furniture or a bottle of nappies that are later deemed dangerous to the people who came into contact with them. finally, i would like to thank you very much for all the support and i hope that we will have a good vote tomorrow and i look forward to the commission's concrete legislative proposal.
for the alde group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. one of the eu's greatest achievements is that we have erased the borders between member states and that we allow people, goods, services and capital to flow freely. it requires trust between member states and between the eu institutions and it requires that we handle external borders wisely. the major challenge for the eu is to ensure trust between countries without building a 'fort europe' for that purpose. many people apply for jobs in the eu. although the eu has historically been attractive to many educated people, this may change dramatically. china and india are now better at attracting educated people, and at the same time we in the eu are scaring them away with complicated bureaucracy and growing xenophobia, and this is serious. today we have an ageing population in the eu and if we do not reverse this trend, by 2015 there will be one worker for every pensioner. the solution is that we need to be proactive and attract well-educated workers and create a system that better takes advantage of immigrants' knowledge, experience and skills. a hundred years ago, europe was a continent that people left to seek better opportunities, like in america. today, the eu is a continent that people come to. we must remember that man is by nature a migrant.
mr president, colleagues, the council conclusions on the financial framework from february in their current form are not acceptable to my group. we now look forward to serious negotiations in order to be able to agree a deal as soon as possible, and i very much welcome the message from minister creighton on this point. so this is not the final decision on the next mff, and it is not a matter of prestige for the european parliament to negotiate. the issues at stake are substantial and substantive. it is about supporting the economic recovery – is that ok for a stimulus, mr mitchell? – and financing the political priorities of the european union for the next seven years. this is a very modest resolution. we do not even challenge the overall level of the budget proposed by the council, in spite of the fact that the proposal is insufficient to ensure a successful implementation of the eu’s most important policies. the proposal is not sufficient to fund the growth and jobs package, not sufficient to fund the europe 2020 strategy for sustainable growth, and not sufficient for the necessary development aid to meet the millennium development goals. so all of this we want to discuss. now, more than ever, we must provide the european union with the necessary and well-targeted means to invest for the future in research and development, in infrastructure, climate and energy policies, improving education, fighting youth unemployment and promoting the eu’s role in the world. we must also provide the union with a budget which is flexible enough to rapidly and sufficiently respond to unforeseen events. this is a budget for the next seven years, and nobody knows today what circumstances we will be facing in 2016 or 2019. therefore, we must have a legally binding mid-term revision of the mff to adapt ourselves to future challenges. we must also avoid a budget deficit. this would not only be illegal but would hamper the implementation of important eu policies like research and education – for example the erasmus programme. it would undermine the credibility of the union. what student, what private company, what university, what researcher will ever apply for, or participate in, an eu-funded project if there is a risk that they will not get paid at the end? finally, for all of those reasons, i welcome the opening of negotiations. i am happy to see that both parliament and the council have expressed their readiness for serious talks and i really hope that we can achieve a good result as soon as possible.
mr president, i would like to thank you. when we used the phrase 'arab spring' in this house earlier, we often quoted condoleezza rice who said: 'we bet on stability to get democracy and we got neither'. i think that we now risk falling back into that situation, so that stability becomes what we want to prioritise perhaps before underlining the importance of respect for human rights and freedoms. we now know that egypt is close to collapse both politically and economically and it is quite obvious that we should help egypt. we cannot guarantee and promote security in the eu if we do not help. i do not think that we should see this as a choice, but we need to be there to care and help, but it is of course extremely important that we receive the message from muhammad mursi and the rest of the muslim brotherhood that this is democracy not only in administrative terms but also in terms of values. we are talking about observers and we are of course a little impressed and happy that the eu is going to be able to bring observers, but that is not enough. an election which is administered with the utmost technical correctness can therefore be completely undemocratic, and it is quite clear to us that the values of democracy must be destroyed.
mr president, i have a question for baroness ashton concerning kidnapping in the sinai desert. what is happening there is awful. while it might be deemed a minor problem, given the situation in egypt generally, i would not share that view. i think that we in the european union can actually help here by assisting the egyptian authorities. what we see now are daily atrocities: tortures, kidnappings and attempts to get money by blackmailing citizens all over the world. so, the next time you talk to the egyptian authorities, i would urge you to try to assist them, because this cannot go on. i would add that there is currently a court case in stockholm in which three people are, i hope, to be tried for kidnappings and attempted blackmail.
member of the commission. − mr president, let me start by saying that some good questions were asked and i will consult high representative cathy ashton to provide you with a good answer to your questions. can i refer to the three main issues in the discussion today? firstly the security situation where french and chadian operations have continued against the jihadist strongholds. clearly, retraining the malian army and getting afisma operational is a challenging task. in the short term, the european training mission is getting underway; there are 21 member states participating and the number is rising. all these efforts are vital given the continued threat of asymmetric warfare. it is equally important to ensure respect for human rights. deployment of human rights observers is crucial to preventing abuses or documenting them. all perpetrators of human rights violations must be held responsible for their actions. secondly, on the development side, the european decision to resume gradual aid development cooperation is carrying its first results. it is important that the country’s normal economic life resume as swiftly as possible and that displaced people feel free and safe to return to their homes. thirdly, political progress in implementing the roadmap in a credible manner and making conciliation on national a reality is vital for long-term stability in mali. in this context, it is important that we follow up carefully on commitments made by the government of mali, including those related to the elections and a national inclusive dialogue. elections alone will not cure the country’s problems. it needs a real effort to rebuild trust in the political system. malian ownership and international support are both crucial. preparations for the international high-level conference in support of mali in mid-may 2013 in brussels are ongoing. the malian government is committed to preparing a national strategy for early recovery which will constitute a basis for its appeal to the international solidarity.
member of the commission, on behalf of the vice-president of the commission/high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy. − mr president, recent developments in ukraine continue to present us with a paradox. on the one hand, the european union-ukraine summit in brussels just over two weeks ago took place in a constructive atmosphere with unequivocal commitments from president yanukovych that ukraine is determined to comply with all three areas specified in the foreign affairs council conclusions of 10 december 2012, namely: firstly, follow-up actions to remedy shortcomings identified in the conduct of the 28 october parliamentary elections; secondly, addressing the issue of selective justice and also preventing its recurrence through comprehensive judiciary reform; and, thirdly, implementation of the reforms defined in our jointly agreed association agenda. if these commitments are fulfilled this would consolidate ukraine democratically and would enable us to sign the association agreement, including a deep and comprehensive free trade area, possibly by the time of the eastern partnership summit in vilnius in november 2013. ukraine is well aware that we need to see determined action and tangible progress over the period until the vilnius summit. this steady process is important as there are a number of milestones between now and the summit. in recent weeks we have worked intensively with kiev at all levels to ensure that there is no gap between our expectations, and to help it deliver on the steps required to get us to signature in vilnius. we have noted some encouraging signals from kiev about a more efficient political steering of the process. on the other hand, we note that the situation remains difficult. the blockage of the rada is a rather graphic illustration of the stay of play. this is particularly disappointing after the encouraging cross-party resolution regarding the european agenda. ukraine needs the authorities to create an environment that is conducive to dialogue as strongly as it needs a constructive opposition that can fully assume its responsibility towards the ukrainian people. meanwhile, there are a number of developments which go contrary to the spirit of the commitments taken at the summit. i already expressed my deep concerns about the recent legal proceedings on the annulment of parliamentary mandates based on contested or selectively applied legal grounds. i wish to note in particular the situation surrounding mr vlasenko. let me say this openly: just as it is important that the work of the verkhovna rada is not being blocked, it is also important that this democratic institution works transparently and predictably following the letter and spirit of its own rules. recent developments underline once more the urgent need for judicial and electoral reforms in ukraine. a recent preliminary judgement of the european court of human rights also identified serious systemic problems in the functioning of the ukrainian judiciary, in particular as regards the separation of powers. i wish here to particularly commend the efforts of the european parliament’s mission headed by former president of parliament cox and former polish president kwasniewski. its determination with respect to addressing the matter of selective justice, notably the cases of yulia tymoshenko and yuriy lutsenko, deserves high recognition. this mission needs and has all our support as its importance cannot be overstated. it is key that this mission will be in a position next month to report that there has been clear progress. we have underlined this in every single interaction with the ukrainian authorities in recent weeks and months. let me be very clear: if we want to sign the association agreement – and i am convinced we do, as it is in our shared interest – the way forward for the authorities is not through bringing more and more disturbing news. the time has come for sending some good news in dealing with selective justice. unless the cases of yulia tymoshenko and yuriy lutsenko are properly addressed and there is sufficient confidence that there will be no more use of selective justice, we can hardly talk about conditions that are conducive to the signing of the association agreement. the european union must remain committed to the defined policy of sequenced engagement with ukraine. we have established an unprecedented objective with ukraine and we hope to be able to achieve it. on its side, ukraine needs to take action on matters that will bring it closer to the european union and reverse the recent backsliding. yesterday’s presidential decree by president yanukovych, including the establishment of a high-level national coordination to oversee the implementation of the necessary steps, as set out in the december foreign affairs council conclusions, the recent statement of the ukrainian parliament and the regulation of the cabinet of ministers on the european agenda’s priorities, show that there is determination in kiev to achieve the objectives. let me recall what i said before. it is important to ensure throughout the process that there is no gap between our expectations. i very much look forward to hearing the views of this honourable house.

in writing. − already in the e-mails we receive from many producers of local beverages as a result of this resolution, it is obvious that it’s very difficult to pin down exactly what is a local, customary or traditional drink. trying to create abstract values or transaction documents around these-difficult-to-define beverages is unwise, not only because it reduces our ability to learn, be influenced by and share each others’ traditional cultures, but also because it is an economically unsound principle. we want our legal objects to be well-defined, and it seems to me that something so closely connected to a group identity or personal experience cannot be done best when it is put in the form of legal obligation imposed on others. therefore i’m against the idea of this resolution, which seeks to confine and lock down the cultural freedom of artisans in traditional beverages.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of pesticides and the use of pesticides in foodstuffs. we social democrats believe that agricultural policy must be more environmentally friendly and generate greater collective social benefits. agricultural aid can only be justified if it generates added value for society at large and is directed towards key actions that the market cannot cope with. we also believe that agricultural policy must be made fairer. the distribution of direct aid between different member states and regions must be more evenly distributed. at the same time, we believe that farmers who have made investments and modernised production in the belief that the eu's quota system and safeguarding regulations will soon end should not be punished for their foresight by repealing previous decisions and extending market governance instruments. moreover, such market manipulation has a clear negative impact on the development and competitiveness of the sector. we also want to emphasise that the decision to maintain the eu's export subsidies is a major setback in the development towards a policy of solidarity with the world around us. on all these points, we believe that the negotiating mandates do not meet our ambition for a greener, fairer and more market-oriented european agricultural policy.
