the rapporteur. - mr president! - what is it? the fact that international trade matters to developing countries is, i think, not a matter for debate. i think we can all agree on that. we know that the benefits of globalisation can, or rather must, be passed on to developing countries, and it is our task and indeed our responsibility to ensure that this happens. there are benefits, but there are also risks, of course. it is therefore urgent that developing countries should be more involved in international trade and that we should take the lead in ensuring that this happens. it is true that free trade with developing countries must have development as its objective. sometimes trade and aid are pitted against each other. it has sometimes been said that trade is not aid. that's a crazy attitude! it is necessary that trade and aid go hand in hand, go side by side. both of these approaches must be present. we must ensure that the trading capacity of poor countries is improved, while recognising that aid is a necessity. i think that this is a matter of peace and a future, as we all agree. we cannot therefore leave this aside as something we bring up on certain verbal occasions, but we must actually state and underline it in all contexts. i would also argue that trade relations with developing countries sometimes require a different approach from us in the eu than trade with economically equal partners. we must ensure that these countries are not locked into a dependence on raw materials, so that it becomes their job. we must accept that they can compete with us on equal terms. this is something that we may not always want to accept even here in the european parliament, but we want to protect our own industries when it is the last resort. i would like to stress the urgency of our daring to stand up for solidarity also in practical work and in trade relations. we must also be mindful that in trade relations we can stand up for human rights and freedoms and that we can ensure that child labour does not exist in any area. we must also, of course, ensure that trade reaches and benefits the whole population, as far as we can, not just those living in a big city or in urban areas, but also people in rural areas and also, of course, women and children who often need to be elevated as those parts of the population that need special support, also in terms of trade policy engagement. finally, mr president, i would like to mention briefly the coherence of development policy, or policy coherence for development, which we have committed ourselves to supporting in practice and to which all our policies, whether they relate to trade, agriculture, fisheries or anything else, should contribute in terms of development in the world. let us not forget this in practical work, in practical political work, and not merely mention it or underline it when a report is on the wall or is being discussed in this house.
mr president, i would like to thank you. banks must have larger buffers and maintain a better quality of capital. the financial crisis has shown that banks are taking too much risk. that is why, unfortunately, we need rules on ceilings so that bonuses are regulated by law. i would have preferred that this could have been solved by the banks themselves, but unfortunately too many bank directors seem to be completely deaf to public opinion. i'm not one of those people who's always trying to "shake" the banking industry. i am glad that parliament supported my proposal to change the nomination process for bank boards to reduce the male dominance in the banking world. each bank should set its own targets for the proportion of women on its board, a form of self-rating. this is part of my draft opinion on the directive on company boards from the committee on economic and monetary affairs. it is a proposal which i noted now also seems to be that of germany and the federal chancellor.

unemployment costs more than the entire eu budget. the international red cross says that large parts of the eu are a crisis zone of poverty and food shortages. in southern europe the situation is so in writing. - we voted today in favour of three reports on the use of the european globalisation adjustment fund. since the creation of the fund in 2006, we have always abstained from voting, because we were against the fund from the beginning, as we do not believe that the fund is the right instrument to help workers affected by structural changes. the fund has now grown, evolved and received its own allocation. it exists and will not disappear for the foreseeable future. we are therefore now taking a stand on each individual case if they meet the very strict requirements for co-financing and long-term employment measures imposed on applicants. in these three cases, we consider that the applications meet these requirements. we continue to support more effective and long-term instruments to increase employment, address structural change and increase labour market flexibility in the context of the 2013 budget and the long-term budget.
in writing. - the european parliament voted today on three reports on the use of the european globalisation adjustment fund. since 2009, i have voted against all proposals to use the fund as a rule. this is because i do not think that the fund is the right instrument to help workers affected by structural change. the fund has now grown, evolved and received its own allocation. it will therefore remain for the foreseeable future. to benefit from the fund, applicants must meet very strict requirements for co-financing and long-term employment measures. all three cases at issue meet these criteria. this includes support for continuing training and support for retraining, all with the aim of helping workers to re-enter the labour market. europe and the eu can respond to competition and structural changes in the market in much better, more efficient and more sustainable ways in the long term. now that the fund is there, it's about making the best of the situation. in view of this, i have therefore voted in favour of the three reports in question.

in writing. − the projections show that greenhouse gas emissions will fall by more than the 21% target for 2020. however, the excessive free allocation of allowances and the decreases in emissions due to the economic crisis have led to an accumulation of surplus allowances with very low prices as a consequence. in the short term, this means that the incentives to invest in environmentally friendly technologies will be reduced. energy prices are high, though. we are sceptical about imposing extra costs on industry in a situation where we are reaching the emission targets. moreover, for the confidence of market participants and third countries in the ets, stable rules of the game are crucial in the long term. we are therefore critical of direct market interventions to influence the price of allowances in the short term when it falls due to weak demand. however, it is important to signal to the market that europe's climate goals are valid and that the ets is the most important tool to achieve the goal. we would therefore welcome a review of climate policy with an emphasis on the period after 2020 in order to ensure a more stable carbon market and that europe reaches the 2050 target.

in writing. − i am in principle against the ets system and a market-driven approach to combating climate change. however, the current reality is that voting against the backloading measures would be a political signal to stop investing in reduced carbon technologies and also send a political signal that climate change policies are no longer a priority. instead, i would like to give a political signal in favour of policies combating climate change, and therefore i have voted in favour of the groote report. but above all, i call for a total revision of the ets system, as it is currently not working as intended.
in writing. the report also calls for the establishment of a european union-wide system of supervision of banks and financial institutions. the european union has been strongly characterised by the neo-liberal belief in deregulation as a path to economic development. the great financial crisis that began in 2008 shows the enormous problems and risks that deregulation has created. to curb the enormity of speculation requires a comprehensive policy. such proposals have been put forward in the econ committee by the gue/ngl group, but were voted down. the left party has also presented in sweden a strong programme of measures to better regulate the banking and financial sectors. a central demand is that banks should be forced to separate traditional banking from speculation. it would powerfully increase stability, and the taxpayers would only have to guarantee the money of small savers, not the speculation of the banks. the kara report has only taken occasional steps in the right direction. i therefore vote in favour of this, but i stress the importance of more far-reaching regulation of the banking and financial sectors.

mr president, i would like to thank you. this is the 18th year without a clean audit report. it's serious and upsetting. however, i think it is almost as serious and disturbing that we have so many people in this house who are almost glad to see it, almost a little glad that you do not get a clean audit report instead of doing what we were elected to do, which is to ask why and, more importantly, what we are doing about it. we know quite well - i learned this last year at least when i was responsible for discharge at the european commission - that the biggest problem is in what is called shared management. a large part of the eu budget is shared between the member states and the commission. shared responsibility is a bit like when my little sister and i had shared responsibility for cleaning our home when we were little. it's like no one did. it's like no one took responsibility. instead, the member states should have had furious debates in council meetings, where they take a hard line against those who misbehave, who they know to be. it is as if it were someone else's money, it is the eu's money, and then you do not claim the same responsibility as a member state. the council and the member states must take their responsibilities. however, the commission also has a responsibility. the commission has a responsibility to take the heavy hand, to suspend programmes, to stop payments, to recover money, to reduce the allocations to regions or member states that are not doing their job. it is not, after all, other people's money, it is our money, the money of the european taxpayers. we should spend more time discussing that responsibility, i.e. how the council and the member states should take that responsibility and how the commission should take that responsibility. after all, every misspent tax penny is a theft from the poor.

in writing. − the annual report of the european court of auditors showed a slight increase in the overall error rate from 3.7 to 3.9 per cent in the implementation of the eu budget. this is troubling. the european commission is ultimately responsible for the use of eu money. however, the court's criticism is not directed primarily at the commission but at the member states. although the commission is formally responsible for implementing the eu budget, the proportion is not due to the commission but mainly occurs in those eu policy areas which are subject to 'shared management' between the eu and the member states (i.e. areas with co-financing, notably the structural funds). the main spending areas of the eu budget, regional and agricultural policies, account for the largest share of the overall increase in errors. we therefore believe that the commission should be given a discharge and that the member states should instead discuss in the council measures to remedy these errors. we also want to point out that we do not want to increase the share of 'own resources' in the revenue system of the eu budget.
in writing. - we voted in favour of the budget for the european parliament, although we are not prepared to defend every detail of the proposal voted on by the plenary. we believe that there is considerable expenditure which is not related to the core activities of parliament. for example, we have repeatedly voted against spending money on our own history museum in connection with the discharge.
in writing. - i voted against the report on the ecb's annual report. the original draft report by the econ committee, named after mep marisa matia, contained significant criticism of the ecb and important calls for greater openness and transparency. it also put forward proposals for alternative ways out of the economic crisis. unfortunately, the right-wing majority of parliament, during the plenary vote, managed to change the content of the report to the extent that mr matias himself withdrew his name from the report. when the content of the report became so clearly a right-wing report, i chose to vote against.
madam president, in less than three months we will have croatian members of this parliament when we start our july session. i think that is an expression of something even bigger than what happens in this plenary, because it is an expression of the fact that croatia will be a member of the european union just like everyone else in this union. that makes the european union bigger but also more important. it is a result of the long development in political leadership that has reformed croatia. it is a membership which changes the situation for croatians, with new opportunities all over europe, but it is also changing the overall opportunities in the western balkans, with a clear sign that membership is possible for countries and nations which are reforming. i think it is an important task for all of us to ensure that, in doing this, we are not creating new borders in the western balkans but new possibilities. it is also important to underline that now we have common tasks ahead: fighting corruption and organised crime – that is important everywhere, and wherever we find it – and of course ensuring that we can achieve the vitality to our economies that will be a guarantee for future prosperity and opportunities. this is a common task, and i think it is important to see that this is a historic step we are taking. thanks to all who have contributed to that.
president-in-office of the council. − mr president, honourable members, thank you once again. when the council assessed progress on enlargement at its meeting on 11 december 2012, as you know, it took a positive view of progress in montenegro towards eu accession. specific issues highlighted included the strengthening of the functioning of the parliament, the judiciary, anti-corruption policy, human rights and the protection of rights of persons belonging to minorities. the council also noted that ongoing constitutional and public administration reforms had advanced. montenegro has also continued to implement smoothly its obligations under the stabilisation and association agreement and to play a very positive role in the region, including through constructive involvement in developing regional cooperation. many of these issues are also reflected in your resolution, and i would like to take this opportunity to particularly thank the rapporteur, charles tannock, for his report and his very positive contribution to advancing montenegro’s accession process. montenegro now needs to build on the progress it has achieved so far and consolidate its reform process. particular attention needs to be paid to the further development of a solid track record in the area of the rule of law. sustainable and lasting reform is needed, in particular with regard to tackling organised crime and cases of corruption, including at senior level. the council has welcomed europol’s report on the issue of organised crime in montenegro. further efforts are needed to ensure the accountability and independence of the judiciary and to strengthen the administrative capacity to implement the acquis. overall, the accession negotiations have got off to a very good start. the cyprus presidency was able to open the first of the negotiation chapters with montenegro, namely science and research. i am pleased to say that the irish presidency has been able, in its turn, to open a second chapter, education and culture, at an accession conference which took place earlier this week. we will do our utmost over the coming months to achieve further progress in the negotiations, including, if possible, the opening of a further chapter. we are satisfied that, in parallel with the negotiations, the screening process is continuing to make very good progress. in december the eu completed its screening of the key rule-of-law chapters, which cover the judiciary and fundamental rights, and justice, freedom and security. montenegro has been invited to prepare the required action plans which constitute the opening benchmarks for these chapters. we do not expect to open negotiations on these chapters during the irish presidency. the preparatory work needed must be of a very high quality, and this means that it will take some time. however, we will continue to encourage montenegro to take this work forward as quickly as possible. this should allow it to tackle these chapters at an early stage in the enlargement process and to start developing a solid track record on the reform agenda, which of course is very important to the council and, i believe, to parliament as well. we would hope to see montenegro submit the required action plans during our presidency in order to allow for consideration of these issues by the council, and we are certainly urging them to do that. so you will appreciate that there is a great deal to be done during our presidency. it is important to keep up the very positive momentum that we have seen in recent months, and i can absolutely assure you of the irish presidency’s commitment to making as much progress as possible in these negotiations. i would like to end by acknowledging and expressing again my appreciation for the work of parliament, and of your rapporteur, in helping to take forward montenegro’s accession process. i very much welcome the close cooperation between our two institutions, and i look forward to continuing to hear your views and taking into account your particular expertise as we move these negotiations forward.
member of the commission. − mr president, i would like to express my gratitude to the rapporteur for montenegro, charles tannock, for this draft resolution. it reflects well the findings of the 2012 progress report on the country. montenegro continues to make great progress on its path towards the european union. the decision to open accession negotiations last june opened a new chapter in the history of the country’s place in europe. in the meantime we have entered the substantive stage of the accession negotiations. the screening process of all chapters is under way. we were able to provisionally close chapter 25, science and research, in december, and chapter 26, education and culture, just three days ago. we have started with the chapters relating to the rule of law, in line with the new approach, and we will maintain this focus. i would at this point like to congratulate the irish presidency for an excellent job done. i would like to make four remarks about the main challenges that lie ahead. firstly, priority needs to be given to the completion of the ongoing constitutional reform to ensure the independence and accountability of the judiciary, in line with the recommendations of the venice commission. finalising this reform will be crucial to making progress in the accession negotiations. secondly, some progress has been made in the fight against corruption and organised crime, but the number of investigations, prosecutions and indictments remains low. thirdly, full compliance with european standards in the area of fundamental rights is essential for montenegro to move ahead with its european integration. the protection of media freedom needs to be ensured. we call on the montenegrin authorities, at all levels, to step up their efforts to investigate and prosecute current and past cases of violence against journalists. fourthly, further efforts are needed to improve the situation of the roma, ashkali and egyptian communities and of displaced persons by ensuring access to their civil and political rights and their economic and social rights. our dialogue with montenegro on these outstanding challenges will continue and intensify during the negotiations. allow me to make a brief remark regarding the recent presidential elections, which are being contested. montenegro is a country negotiating for european union membership. any complaints need to be dealt with swiftly. the election result must be beyond reproach. i am confident that montenegro’s political leaders will take measures to reinforce the credibility of public institutions as well as their independence from party political interests.
for the ppe group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. commissioner, what is it? the day before the decision was to be taken on whether montenegro should be allowed to enter into negotiations or not, i sat down and talked to some of the people who were going to decide on this. at that time there were some who said that montenegro had not completed its reforms, that the fight against corruption and organised crime was not at the level we would like to see when a country becomes a member, and that there was still a lot to be done in terms of various reforms. my argument, and that of a few others, was that this is precisely why we should start negotiations. it is then that we will have the greatest opportunity to influence the country and bring about the changes we want to see. and then the country started negotiating, and that's exactly what happened. thanks to your genius, commissioner, in deciding that all future negotiations should start with chapters 22 and 23. i know that you did a great deal in many countries during your term of office, but if there is one thing i will remember you for it is this decision, because i think it was the absolute best thing that could have happened for the whole region and for all the countries that are starting negotiations. not to punish them, but to give them the opportunity to have sufficient time over a long period to bring about these changes. as regards the presidential election, i would agree with what mr charles tannock has just said. the oece commission has had a report, and according to that report the elections were free and fair, and so far i have not seen any other official report than this one. i would urge the parties and both candidates to accept what was the official result and to resolve the remaining ambiguities in a constructive way and within the institutions, and only within the institutions, because that is how they will demonstrate both their own political maturity and that of their country. that is what will ultimately count when we begin to see montenegro seriously as a full member of our family.
mr president, i would like to thank you. madam secretary! commissioner, what is it? since last year's report on serbia, a lot has happened in the country. we have not only seen new elections and a new government, we have also seen a lot of reforms. the new government has shown the ability to deliver concrete results in cases where previously it only showed its commitment in words. our report highlights a number of areas where further improvements are needed, but it sends a clear and positive signal to serbia that we recognise the progress it has made. we call on the council to start negotiations with serbia as soon as possible and to set a date. not because we think that all the work is done, but because we think that the best way to correct what needs to be corrected and to do what needs to be done is to start negotiations. some of the legal reform has now been implemented and the new laws have actually come into force this week. we will monitor its implementation closely so that there is no room for misinterpretation or abuse of the rules, as was sometimes the case in the past. finally, i would like to say that i support the continuation of the dialogue between kosovo and serbia. i think it was very wise for the european commission to extend the time for the publication of the commission's report and to give serbia and kosovo a little more time to take advantage of this historic opportunity and to reach a solution which would be in the interest not only of serbia and kosovo, but of the whole of the balkans, and indeed in the interest of the eu as well.
madam president, parliament has in its historic vote on reform of the common fisheries policy actually shown that it really is responsible – a lot more responsible in fact than the council, which has systematically decided on tacs which are so much higher than scientific advice that today, some 70 % of europe’s fish stocks are overfished. that is not a great result. we need the long-term management plans. i really appreciate the efforts made by the irish presidency, but we cannot start all over again with each new presidency. the council as a whole needs to be responsible and to show the people of europe that it can in fact manage on decision-making. the lisbon treaty gives us co-decision on long-term management plans – they are the cornerstones of the future cfp, and if we want to win back the confidence of european citizens in the eu, this would in fact be a good example. i really think that the irish presidency should take up the possibility of finalising this.
rapporteur. − mr president, i would to like to thank all the shadow rapporteurs, because this negotiation procedure has not been the traditional ‘i want this, you want that, let’s meet in the middle’. no, it has truly involved listening to each other, integrating each other’s proposals and – after months of deliberations – finally finding a proposal that is workable and highly economically efficient. the aim of this is to deal with the massive market failure that is the vast majority of ships being scrapped illegally, in contravention of the 14-year-old basel ban on the export of hazardous waste. this report will feature a financial instrument that will make it profitable for shipowners to scrap their ships legally. the money collected will go back to the sector if they follow the law. therefore, its aim is to make the market work well again and to protect workers and the environment. there has been a massive lobbying campaign against this proposal by the ports, but it is actually based on incorrect information. i will make a complaint with reference to the lobbying rules on this, because a lot of information has been circulating that is not correct. they have been warning against huge administrative burdens, while the impact assessment said that this is a very efficient administrative way to deal with this problem. they have been claiming that port costs would increase massively, up to 167%, while the true figure is 3.2%. so, please support these financial instruments – amendments 31 and 32 – so that we can negotiate with the council, which stands ready to negotiate with us.

in writing. - i voted in favour of the progress report on turkey, which gives a balanced picture of the state of eu-turkey relations. turkey has made considerable progress, but challenges remain. i therefore support the implementation of the 'positive agenda' to push forward the necessary reforms. among other things, it is important that turkey's judicial system be made more transparent and that the new constitution of turkey be drafted in an open and democratic manner to guarantee equal rights for all citizens of turkey. i am particularly committed to freedom of expression, including online, and welcome the fact that the report highlights the problems of imprisoned journalists and long periods of pre-trial detention. i also support visa liberalisation with simplified procedures between the eu and turkey, especially for entrepreneurs, academics and students. another important step forward is the ongoing constructive dialogue to reach a peaceful solution to the kurdish question. i am glad that we also voted to open negotiating chapters on regional, social and legal issues as soon as possible. at the same time, more reforms are needed in turkey to address key issues such as violence against women, minority rights, media freedom and, not least, relations with cyprus.

mr president, i would like to thank you. i am glad that parliament is able to take a strong stand today in the fight against tax evasion. i am also pleased that both of parliament's reports are so clear about the link between reduced tax evasion and higher growth. in fact, the fight against tax fraud, tax evasion and aggressive tax planning is crucial to generating long-term sustainable growth. on the one hand, reduced tax evasion makes for healthier public finances. more budgetary space is being created for forward-looking investments in education, research, infrastructure and the green transition that are now needed to get the wheels of the european economy turning. on the one hand, a reduction in tax fraud helps to create a level playing field where shady companies are not allowed to take advantage. this effect should not be underestimated. a prerequisite for positive economic development is that serious entrepreneurs are given a fair chance to compete. it is now up to the heads of state or government to deliver at the summit tomorrow. they have every opportunity to get their hands on the cheat. parliament's reports show that there are a wide range of effective tools to be used. it's just a matter of political attitude. it's all about attitude. with the right political will, excellent results can be achieved.
mr president, i would like to thank you. as people across europe feel the effects of welfare cuts, it is provocative that many of the largest companies, funds and foundations have questionable schemes to avoid taxes. taxes should be reasonable, but they should be paid in solidarity. tax rates vary from country to country, but this is natural because welfare is financed in different ways. however, it is important to finally come up with a common corporate tax base. i see, mr president, that the speaker after me is mr hökmark, and i can assure you all that we are, at least, two of those paying the highest rates of income tax in this house.
mr president, we have been discussing the crisis and how to get out of it. it is important to state that growth is the way to get out of this crisis. that is one of the very many reasons to fight tax fraud and tax evasion, not only because it is criminal behaviour but also because it creates unfair conditions for competition and investments and because it means that investments are, unfairly, being made in other parts of the world rather than in the european union. but i think it is also important to state that we need to have taxes that do not give rise to tax evasion and investment flows to other parts of the world. we need to have competitive taxes that foster growth, investment and new jobs. this means that company taxes or income taxes must enable us to see economic development, investment and growth here in europe. i would like to say to the commissioner – in respect of taxes, among other things – that what we have seen is that financial transaction taxes do not help; they give rise to tax evasion. i can assure you – and we have discussed this before – that we have very clear experience of this from sweden but also from other parts of the world. we must not have taxes that can be abused by those who would wish to evade them. we must have competitive taxes, and we must focus on growth here in europe. we need to have stable and balanced public spending which can foster competitive taxes. increased public spending is not the way out of the crisis; it has been shown to be the way into deeper crisis. we need to have strict public finances and public spending which allows for competitive taxes, then we will avoid tax evasion and foster economic growth in the europe we love. blue-card answer to ms podimata. – i would like to thank ms podimata for the question, and i would like to tell her a secret: it is always the taxpayers who pay the taxes and it is always the citizens who pay the taxes, whether we are bank customers and wherever we are. however, i would like us to have fair taxes which stimulate job creation and investment in europe, and which stimulate european companies to invest more in research, science and production in europe. i would like to have taxes that foster more growth. that is why i think we need to look at these issues. we need fair measures in relation to tax collection in our member states. we need fair and balanced taxes, and i hope that ms podimata will agree with me on that point. it is a good argument against the financial transaction tax and some other solutions built on the belief that we can solve all the problems through higher public spending and higher taxes. that is not the case.

blue-card question. – i have the most sincere respect for my dear friend wolf klinz, but i must ask him, is it not dangerous to divide europe even further at this stage? is it not dangerous? do we not need an inclusive way of dealing with all countries, all 27 (soon to be 28) member states? would you not agree that it would actually be better to include countries like my own, sweden?
mr president, i would like to thank you. there is a lot of fine talk here in the session about why we should have a banking union, but nothing really shows that central supervision would be better than national supervision. bad laws and regulations are just as bad whether decisions are taken at central or national level. instead of a banking union, each country should do two other things: first, to require the banks to split their activities; one part being the traditional banking activity and another being the speculative activity. firstly, we should increase controls on financial markets. they have been deregulated for a very long time in a very irresponsible way, and this deregulation has also led to the financial crisis that we have now. with these two proposals, bank separation and re-regulation, there would really be stability in the financial sector and ordinary people would be able to avoid paying for the casino economy of the bankers.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the banks can no longer be effectively supervised at national level. i therefore have a completely opposite view to that which mikael gustavsson referred to earlier. we need a single strong european supervisory authority, not 27 or 28 weak ones. in a time of economic uncertainty, the european central bank is the most important stabilising factor in europe. what would europe be without the ecb? we need common rules and methods for dealing with banks in crisis, perhaps a bank bailout, as we have had good experience in my country, and a common deposit guarantee. unfortunately, mr president, sweden has chosen to remain outside the banking union, at least for the time being. i regret that and so does the ruling party i represent. swedish banks were in trouble in the 1990s. i was a member of parliament when interest rates were 500% and on the way up to 2000% before the currency became liquid. memory is short, and today europe's and sweden's banks are international. there is no guarantee that sweden will not be hit again, and sweden should therefore join the banking union. all countries participating in the ssm joint supervisory mechanism shall have the same rights and obligations. i think there are proposals that are going in the right direction. finally, i would like to put a question to mr giegold. what would your advice be to the swedish government and your green friends in stockholm? should they join this ssm or should they be left behind and actually not be a part of it? i think we are listening to you, sven, in stockholm.
mr president, i think it is important for us to have more stable banks, better supervision and better ways of recovering and resolving banks. i think it is too easy to believe that we can solve these problems just by institutional reform. it is not that easy, and sometimes when i listen to the discussion, it seems like some colleagues and friends believe that we are making something at european level that will solve the problem. that is not the case. i think, first of all, that we need to use the union we already have – that is, the european union – which is dealing with banking legislation in a number of areas. i think we should welcome the fact that we have the new directive on capital requirements, because it will increase the stability of banks. we have a process and negotiations with deposit guarantee schemes, which is important for depositors and for security in the banking market. we had the opportunity yesterday to reach a decision in the committee on economic and monetary affairs of the european parliament on the directive on the recovery and resolution of banks, and we now have a mandate to start negotiations with the council as soon as the council is ready. we welcome that. i think these pieces and cornerstones are important to remember when we discuss how to proceed. i think it is important that we can bring about a better and stronger supervisory regime for cross-border issues. i think the common supervisory mechanism we are talking about is a contribution to that. however, i think at the same time that it is also important that we proceed without creating a new fragmentation or division – neither a political division, nor a fragmentation of the market – because, in spite of our objective of strengthening supervision, it is important that we do not undermine the things we have, and also that we can mobilise the strength of the european union economically, financially and politically. i welcome the report and the opportunities as regards the non-euro members. i would, however, like to stress one thing: when we proceed, let us assess what we have achieved already and ensure that we are not creating new fragmentation and division, because we need stronger and better supervision.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it is a good thing that we have now reached a final decision on the new ecb supervision. this is a major step forward in the stabilisation of the banking sector. at the same time, we must be clear that much remains to be done. the practical operation of supervision will require consistent efforts over a long period of time. first, we must ensure that the ecb's supervisory function is given sufficient resources to be able to act effectively. it must not be undersized. a major factor behind the crisis was that several national regulatory authorities were too small to do a good job. they fell into the lap of powerful banking interests. secondly, we must ensure that the ecb becomes a constructive, cooperative player in the european supervisory work. the ecb shall contribute to convergence and integration not to fragmentation. the ecb must respect the eba's overall responsibility for banking supervision in the eu. the ecb must also respect the competence and experience of the national supervisory authorities. thirdly, we must ensure that the ecb's supervision is really characterised by transparency and dialogue with elected representatives and citizens alike. in order to build this necessary legitimacy, the new supervisory function must be transparent, clear and consistent in its actions. ecb supervision has all the potential to become a major asset, but this requires reasonable conditions and committed scrutiny and debate.
for the alde group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i, and i'm sure most of us here tonight, regret that reading among our young people seems to be declining year by year. but the very thought of no longer having the natural and unrestricted access to the world's literature is appalling to me. unfortunately, this is a painful reality for many, many visually impaired people in the eu today. for me and for the liberal group, it is obvious that we should support access to literature for the visually impaired, and i really hope that agreement will soon be reached on an international agreement within the framework of wipo to facilitate access to literature for the visually impaired. we should remember the copyright. it exists to protect creative creation, to safeguard quality and to enable writers, poets, playwrights and others to make a living from their literary work. however, copyright must never be an obstacle to the right of visually impaired persons to have direct, immediate and equal access to literature. i and the alde group share the concerns that several colleagues have raised earlier about articles d and e, and i hope that commissioner barnier here tonight can bring clarity to this and that parliament's views will be taken into account in the final negotiations on the agreement which i hope will be a success for us all. anything else would be deeply unworthy of our union.

member of the commission. − mr president, i would like to thank the committee on international trade, and in particular its rapporteur david martin, for their excellent cooperation on the commission’s proposal to reinstate preferential treatment to myanmar/burma. this reflects the great progress made by this country in its economic and political reforms, including in the field of labour rights. duty and quota-free treatment will contribute to the country’s economic development, as has already been the case with other beneficiaries of this scheme. in 1997 myanmar/burma’s access to our generalised scheme of preferences (gsp) was temporarily withdrawn due to serious and systematic practices of forced labour, in contravention of the international labour organisation’s core convention on this issue. but since 2011 the country has been undertaking a programme of openness and reform, which has brought unprecedented positive results, and work to further raise standards continues. on 20 april 2012 this parliament called on the commission to consider allowing myanmar/burma privileged market access to the eu. the same month the foreign affairs council gave guidance recommending reinstating the country to the gsp as soon as the ilo marked progress regarding forced labour. on the same occasion the eu suspended restrictive measures for one year, which have all now been lifted except for the arms embargo. in the meantime, in june 2012 the ilo conference recognised the progress of myanmar/burma towards eradicating forced labour, and lifted restrictions which excluded the government of the country from receiving ilo technical cooperation. this allowed the commission to reassess the legal conditions for withdrawal of preferences and to present a proposal on 17 september 2012 to reinstate gsp benefits to myanmar/burma. the commission expects that preferences will help increase exports and the income of people in myanmar/burma. we took the decision to apply the regulation retroactively as from 12 june 2012 to allow eu economic operators to engage early enough with the country. judging by the interest received, we have indications that this is happening. this brings me to an important consideration that lies at the core of the resolution this parliament will adopt, and which rightly points to the fundamental role of the government of myanmur/burma in the continuation of the process of full democratisation, consolidation of the rule of law and respect for human rights and freedom in the country. you can most certainly make a significant contribution through development aid, but this will not be enough. in addition, it is important that eu businesses actively contribute to the national effort to establish better living conditions for all. i therefore welcome the emphasis on corporate social responsibility standards in the resolution. there have hardly been any business relations for eu operators with this south asian nation. in this period of new engagement, sustainability of reforms and livelihoods must also be the main focus of eu business interests in the country.

in writing. - i have voted on the report. the type of subsidised housing (social housing) that the report deals with has contributed to a highly segregated housing in many of the eu member states. nor is it a long-term solution to the housing crisis. the report is about emergency aid, not about housing as a basic social right. what the eu needs is a new economic policy that focuses on growth and investment. such policies create new housing and new jobs. it is the job of society to build affordable housing for all and to create opportunities for good and affordable housing, not segregation. it is on the basis of such reasoning that i choose to abstain.
in writing. - in the vote on the report on subsidised housing in the eu, we voted in favour because we support the wording of the report that 'having somewhere to live is a fundamental right'. the report takes account of the treaty of lisbon (tfeu), in particular article 3 (3). there's too few housing being built. today, the major population growth is occurring in cities and other urban areas. subsidising housing construction is not desirable, but it is necessary to have affordable housing. in line with the principle of subsidiarity, the forms of subsidised housing are to be determined by the member states. it is important to stress that housing policy is not a european issue, but a matter for the member states to decide. subsidising housing is a means of increasing construction, but it is also important to increase competition, and the right of municipalities to set local building requirements is limited. building standards should be revised to allow for smaller dwellings with a slightly simpler standard. solutions that reduce the cost of building accessible should be pursued. in order to reduce the planning times for construction projects, the appeal process shall be simplified.
the rapporteur. - mr president! - what is it? yes, we are making history today. the work on the common asylum package began in 1999 and now, 14 years later, it is becoming a reality. it has been a long road, not always an easy one, and at times this day has felt very, very far away. but because all of us who have worked together towards this goal have been driven by the same shared conviction that our approach to refugees must be improved, we have now reached the goal. the asylum package has become a reality and a creation of which we all have reason to be proud. with the new laws we are adopting, we are creating a common regulatory framework and setting a minimum level to which the member states now have to comply. the long process has resulted in many compromises between our institutions. i am both pleased and proud that the new rules are much clearer, more humane and much more legal for asylum seekers and also clear for the member states. i want to thank everyone who has worked together for a good collaboration. at times, we've spent more time with each other than with our families. i would like to thank the council, the six presidencies with which i have worked, all the experts, assistants and officials. we are now in the final stage, and there are now clear, clear deadlines for the different steps in an asylum process and a clear division of responsibility between member states, also in cases where a person is to be transferred between countries. this prevents protracted proceedings and people from being in legal limbo when no one is responsible for investigating their case. other major improvements include the right to information, the right to legal representation, the right to appeal, the limited possibility of locking people up and significantly improved conditions for unaccompanied refugee children. as the rapporteur of the parliament on the new dublin iii regulation, i would like to mention in particular some progress in my report and the very new paragraph which states that transfers may not be made to other member states if the person is at risk of inhuman or degrading treatment. if there are grounds for suspecting this, the application for asylum or the asylum examination shall be made in the country where the person is present. furthermore, it creates an expanded possibility for unaccompanied children to be reunited not only with parents but also with siblings, grandparents, aunts and uncles who are legally in one of our member states. we have also set up an early warning mechanism to monitor how member states implement the legislation. the asylum office in malta will, together with the commission, play a key role. the aim is clearly to detect problems in the asylum systems of the countries at an early stage. the mechanism is designed to prevent more countries from collapsing. the final point in the negotiations was where to process the asylum application of the unaccompanied refugee child. the dublin ii regulation has not been clear on this point. we wanted to clarify with the commission that unaccompanied children should not be subject to transfer, but should have their asylum applications processed in the country where they are. the council did not want to agree to this. that is why we are making a declaration which will be published tomorrow together with the dublin iii regulation, with a reference to a case before the court of justice of the european union on the asylum procedure for unaccompanied minors, to which the commission will return with a modification of this article in accordance with the judgment of the court of justice delivered on thursday, 6 june, and which states very clearly that unaccompanied refugee children should not be subjected to transfers but treated in the country where the child is present. i'll end where i started: we're making history, we all have a reason to thank each other and be proud!
mr president, i would like to thank you. i welcome the asylum package, but i'm not jumping for joy and waving flags. there are still major shortcomings. one of the main ones is that the possibility of locking up asylum seekers remains. in large parts of the union, asylum seekers are being treated as prisoners and locked up in a system of lock-downs in violation of international law. that we couldn't change this more is tragic. in the dublin regulation we have fought to stop the transfer of unaccompanied minors between member states. the council refused to agree to this but must now comply with the judgment of the court of justice of the european union. today's review also does nothing about the question of the skewed distribution of responsibility for asylum seekers. a large number of member states are watching, while a minority are accepting almost all applications. if these member states took greater responsibility, the situation of refugees would be considerably improved and the eu as a whole could help more people on the move. finally, the possibilities for legally entering the eu and applying for asylum must also be much improved. we have a lot of work to do.
mr president, i also welcome very much the new asylum package we have all worked for. the common rules, minimum standards and procedures in the european union are win-win for all, for the north and for the south. i would particularly like to welcome the progress and improvements for unaccompanied minors. solidarity has to be european, and we have instruments for it and it is very important. but some countries need to build a national capacity to stand up for our values and for the human rights that asylum seekers deserve. common rules will give legal certainty, consistency and better conditions for asylum seekers. but a country like sweden ranks number three in those receiving asylum seekers; 90% of asylum seekers are coming to nine countries. we need to have wider burden-sharing – and it is not a burden, my friends, i do not like to use the word ‘burden’. it is time to stop talking about burden-sharing and start talking about responsibility-sharing, and solidarity goes with responsibility. it is time to stop considering asylum seekers that flee war and conflict and torture and persecution as a burden. they are human beings in need of protection and we have to live up to this. in sweden we want to continue to live up to it, but we need others also to bear their responsibility.
the rapporteur. - mr president! - what is it? by building this common asylum system now, we can give more people a proper protection. together, we can help more people and help better. today, as we know, only a few countries receive almost all those seeking asylum in the eu. we must now work under the banner of 'shared responsibility, mutual trust and increased solidarity'. it's been a long road, as we said before, but the hardest part is ahead. the legal framework is in place. it is now up to us to implement this framework, these laws, correctly. it's very important. in the future, it will no doubt be necessary to review and revise the rules repeatedly. our contribution, this common asylum package, is crucial for a sound and coherent asylum policy in the eu. i would like to say a little more personally that we have managed to create this now in the midst of an economic crisis, which is what we failed to do in 1999 and since then when the economies were booming, and that this shows that the eu is bigger than its own reputation, more active than the ideas about the eu, and that the union has every reason to be proud of the way in which we now meet those who, for various reasons, are forced to flee their countries to seek refuge here in our member states. we have every reason today to be humble in our gratitude and pride in taking our share of responsibility for some of the suffering in the world.
mr president, i would like to thank you. first of all, let me thank commissioner barnier and the two rapporteurs for the flexibility and sensitivity they have shown during the work process. the legislation we are about to adopt represents the hope that billions of dollars of commodity industry revenue can be shared by ordinary people in developing countries, instead of falling into the pockets of corrupt leaders, and the hope that transparency and accountability can replace corruption and nepotism. similar rules have been adopted in the united states. perhaps this will be the beginning of good international standards. this legislative package is also a testament to successful lobbying. it is not a question of how much money you have, but of what arguments you have and with what fervour and credibility you present those arguments. i myself remember with joy and gratitude a meeting i had with bishop munga of tanzania on this subject. it was a meeting marked by respect and care for the most vulnerable, namely people in developing countries who have suffered because we have not had transparency in our processes when european companies have acted in developing countries in particular. i am very happy and think we should all be proud if this is now adopted. it will serve as a model for similar reports in the future.
mr president, i would like to thank you. we know that some european companies are behaving badly, particularly in the poor part of the world. they promote corruption, engage in tax evasion, or manage natural resources irresponsibly. it is therefore positive that the new rules on country-by-country reporting are now in place. the increased transparency requirements will force companies with questionable activities to do better. at the same time, the fact that the rules only apply to the oil, gas, mining and forestry sectors is a clear shortcoming. the problem is not confined to a few industries, but is found in most places. it is therefore important to go directly further and extend the rules to cover all sectors. the rules should be made comprehensive. we in parliament should be very active in pushing that process forward. of course, i would also like to thank the rapporteurs and all those who have worked on this.
on behalf of the ppe group. – mr president, may i first of all thank mr sánchez presedo for his work on the competition report for 2011. competition policy and its relationship to the overall competitiveness of our member states, and the union, is of fundamental importance. that is why i welcome very much that this report that we are discussing today will tomorrow mean that we decide on a number of important messages in regard to increasing competition in sectors of our economies where the level of competition is insufficient today. first there is a clear message on the importance of making sure that competition policy furthers the interests of consumers and new entrants, rather than companies that are already established enterprises. that is important. secondly, the call for more competition among providers of services of general economic and social interest: here we are dealing with some of the most important services in our societies and it is important that consumers, citizens, can make a choice and that they can get the best of the best possible. this is crucial because, if we want to make these services more efficient and consumer-oriented, we must secure the plurality and ever ongoing development of these services. thirdly and finally, today too many services are exempted from scrutiny by the competition authorities, not least in relation to services of general economic and social interest. it is important that the commission always takes an overall perspective on how to further these interests, because what is important in one area is important in another area as well. when consumers can benefit from competition in one sector of our economy, they can also do so in another sector. this is a core issue of the european union, not only for economic purposes but also for opportunities for new thinking, new ideas in the development of our europe.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i welcome the commission's decision to put forward a proposal for collective redress, so that individual citizens and businesses can enforce their rights under eu law when they have been infringed. i am a strong advocate of a european type of group action and welcome this first important step. this is necessary for the functioning of the internal market to strengthen consumer interests. i would have liked to have seen a common european law. we know that consumers and small businesses are afraid to initiate proceedings because of concerns about lengthy procedures and, above all, high costs. it is very important that the commission now pays particular attention to the energy sector. the shale gas revolution in the us is threatening european competitiveness. the eu needs a common energy market, which is efficient, flexible and competitive. we need it to cope with the competitive and difficult energy market of the future.

in writing. - i welcome the adoption of the 2014 calendar and the fact that we will continue to have sessions in brussels. i voted against the amendment which sought to remove the april, october and november partsessions in brussels and to have only partsessions in strasbourg. i believe that the amendment would contribute to the removal of the european parliament from brussels and thus weaken the democratic control of its members and their ability to act fully as a co-decision-making institution, as provided for in the lisbon treaty. i will continue to actively campaign for a single seat for the european parliament in brussels and put an end to the moving carousel!
in writing. − i voted in favour of the directive laying down common standards for the reception conditions of asylum seekers. the new rules will provide higher and more harmonised standards of living for applicants of international protection across the eu, irrespective of which member state in which the application was made. i am particularly happy about the new rules concerning the special reception needs for vulnerable persons such unaccompanied minors and victims of torture. this new directive, together with the other instruments of the asylum package, is an historic step forward to increase the protection of asylum seekers and refugees in europe and to provide consistency, legal certainty and humanity dignity across the eu.
in writing. − i voted in favour of the revision of the dublin regulation, because in its new form the mechanism to determine which member state is responsible for an asylum application will be more effective and simplified. moreover, the new rules will increase the protection of asylum seekers and provide for better legal certainty and human dignity. i think that the new early warning mechanism introduced in the revision will help to prevent crisis situations and assist member states by better using the instruments that we already have. the new article 3 that refers to a risk of serious violation of the fundamental rights of the person concerned is also important for speeding up decisions, whereas before there was the need to wait for a court ruling to stop a transfer. this regulation, together with the other instruments of the asylum package, is an historic step forward that will increase the protection of asylum seekers in europe and allow better responsibility-sharing among member states.
in writing. − i am glad that we have adopted today new common rules on asylum procedures in the eu. there are still too many differences in the national asylum systems across europe today and we need common rules to ensure more consistency and legal certainty. the new procedure directive simplifies and harmonises definitions and time limits, reducing the divergences and margins of error in the interpretation by member states. i supported in particular the improvements achieved for unaccompanied minors. the best interest of the child is the first priority throughout the whole package. this directive, together with the other instruments of the asylum package, is an historic step forward that will increase the protection of asylum-seekers in europe and allow a better responsibility-sharing among member states.
in writing. - i voted against the report. the eu's neoliberal austerity policies have created mass unemployment and growing poverty. what the eu needs is not charitable funds for the poor, but a new economic policy that creates jobs and welfare. i have voted against the report on this basis.
in writing. - we have today voted to abstain in the final vote on the report on the fund for european aid to the most deprived. we want to show all our solidarity with the people who are the most disadvantaged, and we are well aware that this group of people has increased in the wake of the economic crisis. however, there are various ways in which these people can be helped. we therefore voted to make this fund voluntary for the member states to use (amendment 73, part 2). however, that line was not supported in plenary, and we decided to abstain. several member states, including sweden, have national programmes to assist the most disadvantaged. in a country like sweden, the limited esf resources should be used for their intended purpose (integration, gender equality and employability). a country like sweden does not need a contribution from the fund either. it would be a pity to have to spend money that could be better used in other member states. we believe that this is a matter for the member states to take care of themselves, but we believe that there should be a fund at eu level which can be used by those countries which so wish.
in writing. − i voted in favour of the new eurodac regulation because i believe that it is important to give law enforcement authorities the possibility of accessing the eurodac database in order to fight serious crime and terrorism. of course we need to ensure strict safeguards for the people concerned and to avoid abuse. the access will be only allowed under strictly limited circumstances. it will not be a general direct access to the database, but only on a ‘hit or not’ basis and as a last resort. it will be requested only in specific cases where there is a crime scene. strict rules on data protection are also provided in particular in relation to the storage period of data. i think that in the new regulation we succeeded in finding the right balance.
in writing. - i voted against the macovei report on the establishment of eurodac for the comparison of fingerprints. the report, and the outcome of the tripartite negotiations, seems to assume that all asylum seekers are potential terrorists and criminals. the proposal to store all fingerprints for three years, including those of refugees who have been granted asylum, is one example. it's a gross violation of privacy. the compromise between the european parliament and the council leads to a deterioration of the standard that has been in force. the other three reports on asylum (by guillaume, wikström and hidalgo) are to be adopted on the basis of the 'approval without vote' principle. i would just like to emphasize that i would have voted against these reports, partly because they further weaken asylum policy and partly because i do not believe that asylum policy should be supranational.
mr president, freedom of movement is a cornerstone of europe, and is worth standing up for. today, with the agreement on schengen governance, we have made it possible to have more transparent, more efficient and stronger cooperation within schengen. 420 million citizens will continue to enjoy the right to move freely without passport control, but at the same time they will have more safety and protection within the schengen area. the key progress made is in moving towards the community method; the approach will be more european and less intergovernmental. there will be a possibility to reintroduce border controls but, as now, only on a temporary, exceptional and last-resort basis. i think that it is important that the criteria for border controls based on increases in migration flows should not be accepted, because that has nothing to do with national security. mr president, today we are sending a message to those who wanted to roll back europe: that we stand firm for freedom of movement, as a right of our citizens that we do not intend to endanger.
in writing. - the left party has opposed the schengen agreement from the outset, both on the basis of our anti-federalist stance and our consistent defence of human rights. the schengen system has contributed greatly to the building of walls around the eu and the creation of a fortress europe. the consequence has been that thousands of refugees have died trying to escape to the eu. the flows of refugees are caused by war and deep economic inequalities in the world, which the eu also helps to create. in line with this reasoning of principle, i am voting against this report.
