vice-president of the commission/high representative. − madam president, i have said many times in this house that human rights should be the silver thread that runs through the statements and actions of the european union. over the last twelve months, thanks in no small part to stavros lambrinidis, who is sitting beside me, and the external action service team led by mara marinaki, we have made significant reforms in our approach, our initiatives and our actions on human rights. last year we adopted our strategic framework and action plan on human rights and democracy, agreed unanimously by member states and supported by this parliament through its high-level contact group on human rights. today we have a human rights focal point officer in every eu delegation, as well as a brussels-based working group in the council. we have fully developed human rights country strategies and a much stronger annual report, which allows parliament and civil society to measure our performance against our commitments. the substantial restructuring of the report makes it a much more effective tool for our ongoing work and, specifically, it responds to the request of parliament by following the structure of the action plan and highlighting concrete achievements and challenges. the report also demonstrates the steps we have taken to increase our interinstitutional cooperation and our policy coherence, visibility and effectiveness across our work. we have made significant progress in these areas. i want like to highlight a few things. first of all, the appointment of the first eu special representative for human rights, under your guidance and my mandate. i would like to pay tribute to his energy, commitment and determination. he has worked with over a hundred ministers and ambassadors across the world and worked closely with you and with teams on the ground to raise the profile of human rights. he has also worked closely with civil society in europe and in other countries, and helped to secure really important changes. the protection of ngos and human rights defenders is a core priority for both stavros and for me. he is actively engaged with over 150 international and local ngos from 30 countries to safeguard their space to function freely and to promote our support. i want to thank him most warmly and look forward to many more achievements in the future. stavros knows better than anyone that the support that we give is not just financial, but also political, legal and diplomatic. in that regard i would like to underline how tremendously important parliament’s sakharov prize is. there is no question that 2012 was a challenging year for civil society in so many countries. there is a worrying trend that parliamentarians here have spotted to limit the space – through legislation, court action and restrictions – of civil society, particularly with regard to foreign funding. i believe that safeguarding the work of civil society is fundamental to the work of the european union. i believe that safeguarding freedom of association and peaceful assembly has to stay at the top of our agenda. i have expressed our concerns about these developments in statements, but also in person. i will continue to do so and, with the support and help of stavros and the work of our delegations, we will continue to try to ensure that civil society is given the space its needs and that governments – old, new, transitional or permanent – understand that this is fundamental to the perception of them and fundamental to the kind of society they wish to create. ngos and civil society make governments and politicians uncomfortable. that exactly is what they are supposed to do: to push the boundaries, to watch over the way governments work and to get under their skin. in 2012 we also saw the great difficulty faced by many who seek to practice their faith and have the freedom of their belief, or none. we make it clear this is an inalienable human right and an essential pillar of society. it is an integral part of our political dialogue with third countries. i have spoken out to condemn religious intolerance and discrimination, whether in egypt, nigeria, iran, iraq, libya, mali, pakistan, tunisia or elsewhere. be in no doubt that our aim in speaking is to achieve results. we know, for example, that the statements made in tripoli contributed to the release of egyptian christians held in libya. the importance of this issue is why we wanted our new guidelines and why we have consulted widely to make sure that they have a strong and clear content. i am extremely grateful again to the parliament’s subcommittee on human rights for their valuable input. i very much welcome the andrikienė report. i trust that many of the recommendations will be reflected in the final version of the guidelines, which will be adopted by the foreign affairs council on 24 june. i have also been extremely worried about the discrimination in some countries based on sexual orientation or gender identity. as you know, we have contributed to multilateral efforts, especially in the un, as well as at regional and bilateral level. here too, we have developed practical eu guidelines to be adopted this month by the council, which should reinforce the work of the european union. we have raised our deep concerns with uganda and cameroon about proposed parliamentary bills further criminalising homosexuality. in malawi we have spoken out against the prison sentences for gay couples and against recent events in nigeria. we use our regular dialogues to raise this discrimination and to promote tolerance, supporting campaigns in russia, croatia, turkey, montenegro and brazil to support local and international ngos in campaigns against discrimination. i thank this house for your input. if i may single out michael cashman, i will do so for his tireless efforts. when we look at our changing world, i also continue to be concerned about what is happening to women and girls. honourable members in this house know that no society can function well and no economy do as well as it should without the input of the rich talent and ability of half its population – women and girls. i was really pleased that we saw the successful adoption of conclusions at the recent 57th session of the un commission on the status of women. but let me say that this was not easy. some countries tried to water down the unconditional condemnation of violence against women through appeals to domestic traditions, laws and cultures. it was the tireless work of our eu delegations that was largely responsible for the defeat of those attempts. we are united in our belief that there are no acceptable circumstances for violence against women and no place where the failure to allow girls to be educated is acceptable. no society can hold its head up where women are forced to keep their heads down. so we continue our strong engagement in the implementation of security council resolution 1325, with support to the tune of eur 200 million a year for the development and implementation of national action plans, funding for ngos and training. we strengthened our commitment further with a memorandum of understanding with un women that forms the basis of a partnership to help us make progress in our international commitments. i am so grateful for parliament’s strong support in this area. i must mention too the focus on freedom of the press and media in the report by ms schaake and welcome it very much. the increased violence against journalists and restrictions on press freedom in many countries, including our neighbours, must be challenged. we condemn attacks on journalists and bloggers and are developing our own guidelines to use in our work in delegations and in our teams in brussels. the commission is already implementing a pilot project, mandated by the european parliament, on a european centre for press and media freedom. the territorial scope will now cover the eu 27, croatia and candidate countries. we also continue with our campaign against the death penalty. i congratulate all those involved in the extensive lobbying which played a pivotal role in the success of the un general assembly 67 resolution. it was adopted with an unprecedented 111 votes in favour and a record 91 sponsors. we should rightly take some of the credit for that. our revised guidelines further refine all aspects of our policy and pull together all of our available diplomatic action. during 2012 and the first part of this year, i issued 54 statements on the death penalty: on breaches of existing moratoriums in india, pakistan, indonesia, taiwan, japan and gambia, on persistent executions in iran, saudi arabia and china and on violations of minimum standards in the united states, malaysia and singapore. during the same period i instructed our delegations to carry out 30 démarches reiterating our position and calling on authorities to refrain from executions. we will continue to push and press for universal abolition. there are new challenges and existing challenges: supporting countries going through transitions, helping them to refrain from restricting human rights and to see that it was for those rights that people have fought and tragically died, working with countries who describe themselves as ‘developing’ so that they develop the right approach to human rights, and, most of all, reminding everyone that for human rights to apply to them, there is only one criterion, which is to be human.
for the ppe group. president. - the debate is closed. freedom of the media is one of the most important preconditions for democracy. that is why undemocratic states are so strongly opposed to free speech. that's why journalists who ask questions, who question and who expose regimes around the world are still being jailed and murdered today. the rapporteur mentioned in his speech a number of countries which are violating the freedom of the media. i can add one more country: eritrea. dawit isaak, a journalist from my own country, has been imprisoned in eritrea for 12 years now. i am glad that we were able to agree across party and national boundaries on our strong support for media freedom in third countries. i am also pleased that we are also mentioning digital and social media in the report for the first time. bloggers around the world have become as much of a threat to regimes as traditional media. they are feared by authoritarian regimes and persecuted to the same extent as journalists. bloggers, but also various sectors of civil society, have recently contributed to significant political, social and economic changes. that is why they deserve our support and protection. i am also glad that we are making clear demands on those countries that want a relationship with the eu, whether they are candidate countries or countries that want to have different agreements with the eu. we demand that they respect the freedom of the media. at the same time, we also demand that the eu be a leader in the protection of journalists and media freedom globally. having said all that, i would like to point out that there are parts of the report which also deal with media freedom within the eu, even though there is a parallel report on media freedom within the eu and even though our report is drawn up by the committee on foreign affairs and, therefore, by definition, is to deal with the situation in third countries. i would like to emphasize that i personally and my political group have always been strong advocates and fighters for media freedom, but we do not want to politicize it. this is a matter that deserves our full attention and our full support, respect and protection for the brave people who risk their lives to ensure that all citizens have the same rights that we exercise here today in this house.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – madam president, i would like to thank the rapporteurs, the shadows and my colleagues from the other committees for the collaboration that we have had on this file. i was happy about many things in the discussions, particularly on the open formats, which were very encouraging and give me some hope for the future. but that said, the commission proposal originally had other aims as well, most of which we did not agree upon, and we did not reach any particular conclusions either. i am afraid that we are now rushing ahead with an agreement for the sake of having an agreement, rather than responsibly and consistently legislating to improve the lives of european citizens. we already have many open data projects in the member states and local authorities. we have heard some data about their successes, but success at the member state level does not necessarily mean that we need an eu law. it is better for us to think this over in another reading, rather than pushing ahead for the sake of pushing ahead. eu citizens’ confidence in our institution relies upon them being able to know that we know when we have a goal and a purpose to make their lives better. the greens/efa and i therefore propose that we reject this trialogue outcome with the council and that we go to a second reading and think this over again. this will not harm the open data project already under way at the member state level, and it would be beneficial for everyone if we took the time to think about what we actually want.
madam president, i would like to thank you. first of all, let me thank our colleague, filip kaczmarek, for an excellent report. there is no doubt that the mdgs have been of great importance to world developments over the past 12 years. the objectives have mobilised, united and inspired both aid recipients and developing countries and they have delivered. it is indeed encouraging to see the success of the programme in several areas. i think what was said earlier here is very important, namely that these successes should be taken forward. there are still a great many people who imagine that this kind of investment is not doing any good and that poverty is almost constant in the world. it's not like that! that message of good news should, to the extent we can, be trumpeted. a total of 600 million people have been lifted out of poverty in the last 12 years. 600 million people! a record number of children are also entering primary school, and now girls and boys are entering primary school in equal numbers. maternal and infant mortality has decreased. targeted investments have been made to combat what might be called public diseases, such as hiv, malaria, and aids, and millions of lives have been saved. we should not underestimate the value of these specifically formulated mdgs as a stimulus and an opportunity to measure progress. i believe that they have been and continue to be of very great value as well. as we look ahead, it is important that we do not stop our discussions at vague wishes or general guidelines for future development work, but that we once again focus on formulating clear objectives of what we want and can achieve together. in new york, kaczmarek's report will be of great use.
in writing. − the epp group supports freedom of press and media in the world, and has voted in favour of the report. the epp group believes, however, that the report should focus on the situation outside the eu, since it is a report that has been drafted within the committee on foreign affairs. therefore paragraph 29 and paragraph 30 were not supported by the group during the vote in committee. paragraph 29 reads ‘notes with concern that in recent years some media, notably in the eu, have come under scrutiny themselves for unethical and allegedly illegal behaviour; considers that the eu can only lead by example if it addresses these issues within its own borders’. paragraph 30 reads ‘encourages the commission to continue to closely monitor the independence of the press and media in the member states’. these paragraphs go beyond the scope of the report. the epp group also voted against the following part of paragraph 8 in committee: ‘considers the trend of concentrated media ownership in large conglomerates to be a threat to media freedom and pluralism, especially with digitisation occurring in parallel’. the epp group is not in favour of drawing these kinds of conclusions, which are questionable and speculative.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. imagine a mob mocking you, taunting you, destroying your house just because of who you were born to or what god you believe in. then imagine that you call the police for help and the police side with the mob, that you have to flee, and that when you do, you will find no safe haven either. this is the reality today for thousands - yes, even hundreds of thousands - of rohingya. and what do we do? we stand here and watch and pass our resolutions. earlier this week, we voted to increase military leadership capacity and to increase the militarization of the foreign service. what good is that to us? no one at all! it is time to take the promises of 1999 seriously and build up the eu's civilian peace mechanisms. we need a task force of mediators, engineers, lawyers, tax experts, bureaucrats, who can build communities, not military men who can bomb communities. it's not going to help this group of people either. i expect the commission to take the initiative now to build up the civilian peacekeeping force, which can help in conflicts and prevent conflicts before they escalate into war. that is where the eu can make a difference. in the meantime, we will make the demands on the burmese president as set out in our resolution. what good is his promise to release all political prisoners? nothing is happening.
mr president, i would like to thank you. you guys! the agreement on the long-term budget is not a good agreement, but it is the best possible one at this stage. we have been criticised a lot, not least by the swedish government, for not having immediately agreed to the council's cow trade in february. but it was really important that we stood up, despite the fact that we were under this very heavy pressure. what, then, have we achieved by continuing to negotiate? we have, in fact, in the next few years, multiplied the joint effort to tackle youth unemployment. we have also found a solution to fill the budget for the years to come. we have had an early postponement of investment in research and small businesses, among other things, which would otherwise have been subject to substantial cuts in the first few years, contrary to the council's promises. we have passed the amending budget which will enable the eu to get out of its payment crisis. we have got a much more flexible budget and, not least, a mandatory mid-term review to develop this long-term budget in a more offensive direction when the crisis is hopefully over. unfortunately, this conflictual process, not least within the council, has revealed a number of shortcomings in the way the eu works. in times of economic crisis, solidarity is weakened. the member states are primarily concerned with their own short-term national interests, such as their own levy. but if the member states do not stand up for what is common, the eu will be weakened and a weak eu is the last thing we need. it is particularly worrying that the member states have tried in every way to reduce the influence of the european parliament in favour of closed-door agreements. we must oppose this, because it is a serious threat to openness and democracy in the eu.
in writing. − harmonised rules on vehicle inspections both increase road safety and reduce the negative environmental impact of road traffic. technology is advancing rapidly. this is reflected in the report, but we do not like the commission's proposal to include two- and three-wheelers. the proposal is based on a poorly substantiated study and therefore we do not think that these proposals should be included in this report. the aim of the proposals can be better achieved at national level. today it is already possible for each country to decide on the compulsory inspection of, for example, motorcycles. mc accidents are mainly due to driver error, road environmental deficiencies or inattentive road users, not technical errors. we want the commission to carry out a better and more comprehensive study on two- and three-wheelers, comparing accident statistics between countries with and without inspection to get a clearer picture of the situation.
in writing. - i voted against the dorfmann report on certain types of overall state aid. although the report aims to simplify the management of grants and subsidies by member states and public authorities, it is nevertheless directly linked to article 107 (1) of the treaty on european union, which prohibits state aid when it is considered incompatible with the internal market. it is a ban which the left party opposes because there are many situations and circumstances in a country's economy where state aid may be necessary for the good of society and its citizens.
mr president, i would like to thank you. thank you, commissioner and sven giegold. ucits is a huge success. we should encourage savings in the form of funds, as this contributes to investment in european companies. therefore, it is particularly important that ucits funds are safe and secure to invest in. i therefore welcome the proposals to regulate the activities of depositaries in a more harmonised way and to strengthen supervision. the savings - my savings and your savings - will be in safe custody. the proposals strengthen consumer protection and level the playing field. of course, the fees paid by the fund investor for managing the fund must be reasonable and fair. of course, there must be full transparency and openness as to the content and extent of the charges. i have one request, however, to sven giegold: let's not let the discussion of bonuses and fees make it so difficult that we don't finish and have a proposal before the end of this term. i think we owe it to the fund savers in europe to get it right.
for the alde group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. thank you to arlene mccarthy. her report and liikanen's report contribute to a debate on how to create a sound banking system in europe. after the financial crisis, the eu has done a lot, not least you, michel barnier, to tighten up the rules and improve the supervision of european banks. we must now implement the eu rules we have developed over the last few years before taking further steps. we need to see the effects of higher capital requirements, rules for banks in crisis and the banking union before we implement new extensive and potentially costly eu rules and rules whose effects we actually don't really know. in many cases, eu rules will be stricter than those of other g20 countries. we must be careful not to undermine the european banking model where normal banking and securities trading are under one roof. let us not risk creating further uncertainty about the legal conditions for banking in europe.
member of the commission. − mr president, a year and a half ago, the commission came forward with a proposal for a decision on serious cross-border threats to health. today we stand on the verge of reaching agreement at first reading. the european parliament, the commission, and the council – under the danish, cyprus and irish presidencies – have all worked very hard to reach such an agreement. the compromise text before us today represents a balanced approach, which reflects the concerns of all institutions. i would like to thank the members of the committee on the environment, public health and food safety, in particular you, mr pargneaux. as rapporteur, you facilitated the discussions between the institutions, and it is thanks to your personal commitment that we have managed to reach a strong compromise. i would also like to thank the shadow rapporteurs for their input into the adoption of this important piece of legislation. i am pleased that the european parliament supported the objectives of the commission proposal and strengthened it further in important areas, such as the need for consistent information across the eu to our citizens and to healthcare professionals; the independence of the experts who carry out risk assessments; and the link between the veterinary sector and the health sector in handling cross-border threats. the commission stands fully behind this agreement and is pleased with the outcome of the negotiations. the draft decision on the table today is based on lessons learnt from past health crises – such as the 2009 h1n1 pandemic, and the 2011 outbreak of e. coli. these cases have shown that europe needs enhanced collaboration to prepare for such crises, and to address them at eu level. this is why the commission identified three major objectives in its proposal on serious cross-border health threats. first, to strengthen preparedness planning in the light of lessons learnt from past experiences. second, to improve risk assessment and management to address all kinds of cross-border health threats; and third, to enhance the coordination of the response at eu level by providing a solid mandate to the health security committee. i believe the decision, as negotiated, fulfils these three aspirations. let me expand a little. first, the decision will strengthen preparedness planning capacity at eu level by reinforcing coordination and sharing of best practice and information on national preparedness activities. the aim here is to ensure that member states are adequately prepared to face a crisis, and to reduce the impact of such a crisis on people’s health and on the functioning of society as a whole. one of the key achievements of the decision is that it establishes the legal basis for the coordination of joint procurement of medicines needed to respond to a crisis. we will start with the joint procurement of pandemic vaccines: the member states who participate in this process, on a voluntary basis, will be better prepared for a future pandemic, and will be able to provide their citizens with vaccines under better conditions than in the past. second, the decision provides for risk assessments on the impact of chemical and environmental threats on people’s health. when no eu agency is suitable to carry out such a risk assessment, the commission now has a clear mandate to provide it. the decision further strengthens the eu position in case of any health emergency, including pandemics. for the first time, the eu can declare an emergency situation within its territory – without waiting for an international emergency to be declared – so as to trigger an accelerated provision of vaccines and medicines. finally, the decision gives the health security committee a solid legal footing. in this committee, member states inform and consult each other to coordinate their responses to cross-border health threats and their communication to the public and to healthcare professionals. the current outbreaks of influenza h7n9 and of the novel coronavirus highlight once more the need for national authorities to exchange information, analyse the risk to citizens, prepare for a possible crisis and coordinate their response. the compromise before us represents a comprehensive and coherent legal framework addressing serious cross-border health threats for the greater safety of european citizens. before i close, allow me to read a statement on behalf of the commission, which has already been deposited with parliament’s secretariat: ‘where a risk assessment concerning a serious cross-border threat to health falls outside the mandates of the agencies of the union, the commission undertakes obtaining such a risk assessment by means of expert groups. the commission will, as a priority, have recourse to the scientific committees established by commission decision 2008/721/ec of 5 september 2008 setting up an advisory structure of scientific committees and experts in the field of consumer safety, public health and the environment and repealing decision 2004/210/ec. ‘ where the specific expertise needed is not immediately available in the advisory structures of scientific committees and where the urgency so requires, the commission will inform without delay the member states and relevant scientific bodies about the particular expertise needed to help identify the experts it is looking for. the commission will then appoint experts to contribute to the risk assessment needed. ‘the commission will ensure the independence of the experts providing this assessment, in accordance with its established internal rules.’ the adoption of this decision is an important milestone for strengthening health security in the european union. this agreement will improve citizens’ safety and we should all give it our wholehearted support.
for the ppe group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. in recent years, we have become accustomed to alarms about new pandemics, such as avian influenza, swine influenza and, most recently, a new, mutated form of avian influenza. all we know is that sooner or later it's gonna happen to us. it is therefore very important that we are prepared when it happens, and that the countries of europe know what is required and are able to coordinate their efforts. that is why i believe that the legislation we are about to adopt is particularly urgent and that we are deepening our cooperation and preparedness against cross-border health threats. this legislation does indeed do that. it strengthens europe's capacity to deal with cross-border health threats. we are strengthening the health committees and the coordination of these committees in the member states. we extend the mandate of the european centre for disease prevention to include crossborder health threats. we are tightening the requirements for member states to establish adequate contingency plans. last but not least, we are also expanding the possibility of using joint procurement to purchase vaccines. i would like to say that i would have liked to see the commission go further, to have had an additional opportunity to intervene when member states are not able to deal with crossborder health threats. because if there is ever a time when we really need the eu, it is when we have cross-border problems. there is no more serious cross-border problem than when we actually have pandemics and health threats that risk crossing borders.

in writing. − like the text of the resolution, we welcome the agreement reached on 27 june 2013. the new longterm budget is, thanks to pressure from the european parliament, much more modern and flexible. eu funds will now be able to be used even more where they are most useful. we also share the criticism of the european parliament of the undemocratic way in which the agreement was reached and the undermining of the european parliament's legislative power under the treaty which the agreement entails. at the same time, however, the resolution sets out a list of new financial requirements which further delay the adoption of the long-term budget which would make it difficult to get a good start on the new 2014-2020 programming period and jeopardise the swedish rebate. we were therefore unable to vote in favour of the resolution as a whole and have therefore decided to abstain in the final vote. we assume that the heads of state or government also share the view that it is contrary to the treaty to leave the eu budget in deficit, and assume that they intend to live up to their commitments in this respect.
in writing. - i have suspended the vote on the giegold report. i strongly support the intention to tackle and regulate this type of speculation which can lead to so-called ponzi frauds. however, on the one hand, the measures should go further than in this proposal and on the other hand, the whole report is based on the idea that this type of financial market is fundamentally a positive thing.
in writing. the swedish social democrats are opposed to three elements of this report. firstly, we cannot support the idea that tax revenues - if and when the tax is implemented throughout the eu - should be used at least in part to expand the eu budget. secondly, we believe that it is wrong to harmonise tax rates completely; eu legislation in the field of taxation should go no further than establishing minimum levels. thirdly, we question the wisdom of making spot transactions in the foreign exchange market subject to the tax. this may in the long run harm the export industry. at the same time, the report also has several positive aspects. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the free movement of goods between the european community and the republic of turkey. in principle, we also see it as very urgent that the undertaxation of the financial sector be corrected. we have therefore voted in favour of the report as a whole.
in writing. - i have suspended the vote on the report by mr podimata. i support the proposal for a financial transaction tax. however, this proposal is not well formulated and is rather like a tobin tax, and the revenue will be passed on to the eu budget. i believe that the revenue should go to the member states' own budgets and be reserved for climate and employment measures and for various forms of aid to developing countries.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover all the activities of the european union. if a tax is to be imposed, it should be imposed globally. the tax increases the cost of capital for companies, which leads to reduced investment and thus lower economic growth. the borrowing costs of governments are also increasing, which must be financed by other tax increases or expenditure cuts. if the tax is to be raised on the financial sector, it should be done in a way that has the least negative impact on the economy. taxing the financial sector would be far better than introducing a tax that hits ordinary citizens' savings, pensions and jobs. we believe that the swedish example of unilaterally introducing a ftt in the 1980s, which resulted in the majority of securities trading moving to london, should be taken into account so that we do not repeat the same mistake at european level. we believe that there is a high risk that the stabilising effects of financial markets will not be achieved if eleven eu countries independently adopt a ftt. major distortions can be expected in the form of transactions moving to less transparent and less open markets outside europe, to non-taxed financial instruments or being discontinued altogether.
in writing. - i have suspended the vote on the balz report. i believe that it is up to latvia itself to decide whether it will join the euro or not. however, i regret that the latvian parliament chose to take this decision without first holding a referendum in which its own people were consulted.
in writing. − i abstained on the report on the situation of fundamental rights in hungary. while i believe that parliament could not ignore or deny the concerns over the hungarian constitutional reform expressed by several independent international organisations, such as the commission, the venice committee and the parliamentary assembly of the council of europe, at the same time i could not support the report in its present form because it goes beyond its mandate and is very intrusive in relation to the subsidiarity of member states. member states should be treated on an equal footing, with no double standards in the monitoring of the application of the eu law. i did not support the new mechanism for monitoring respect for fundamental rights in member states proposed in the report, because i think that the commission, as guardian of the treaties, should be the institution responsible for assessing breaches in eu law. i agree, nevertheless, that procedures could be more effective when it comes to violations of fundamental rights in member states. this parliament can play an important role in bringing the right pressure to bear, but it should not become the judge itself.
in writing. - the european parliament voted today on the situation of fundamental rights: standards and practices in hungary, i chose to abstain. i did so because i do not believe that the european parliament should see it as its task to accuse, judge and execute. the principles and tasks of the rule of law are, of course, to be respected by all eu member states. in the absence of such a procedure, it must be for the commission and, ultimately, the court of justice to act and to give judgment. it is hardly reasonable to build up additional mechanisms in addition to the enforcement efforts that exist today in the field of eu activity.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environmentally friendly use of energy. however, we have doubts about paragraphs 9 and 14 of the report, both of which concern the harmonisation of criminal law within the eu. we believe it is important that the criminal justice systems of the eu member states include a common definition of fraud and irregularities in order to protect the financial interests of the union and to achieve better coordination against fraud within the eu. however, paragraphs 9 and 14 express the need for the harmonization of the member states' criminal laws in all areas, which is a message that we swedish social democrats are opposing, since criminal law is a sensitive area and the views on what should be criminal, how to combat crime and which penalties should be applied differ between the member states. we therefore voted against these points.
in writing. - the fight against fraud is particularly important in the current economic crisis. in 2011, over 400 million euros disappeared. every euro lost through tax evasion, subsidy fraud, smuggling and customs fraud has to be paid for by eu citizens. national regulatory authorities have a major responsibility. e-government, electronic transactions and common it systems for customs authorities facilitate monitoring and increase transparency. this costs money, but the commission should allocate additional resources to national police forces and to europol, so that we can beat the criminal gangs and ensure that the much needed revenue does not go into the pockets of the bad guys.
in writing. the european parliament has also called for the eu to be given a role in the fight against terrorism and for the eu to be given a role in the fight against terrorism. we are, however, skeptical about certain passages in the resolution. in recital f, incorrect information is given that signals intelligence in sweden is carried out 'without legal orders'. under swedish signals intelligence legislation, fra requests authorisation from the defence intelligence tribunal for each new mission. amendment no 3 provides that edward snowden be granted asylum in the eu. however, we believe that any asylum decisions should be made in accordance with the relevant legislation in the member states, and who is granted protection status should not be decided by the european parliament. although we believe that it is essential to clarify the extent of the us surveillance, we do not believe that an inquiry in the european parliament as proposed in paragraph 16 is the right way to go. for example, parliament has no right to request classified documents from member states. moreover, such an inquiry risks duplicating the investigations currently being carried out by the european commission. we therefore abstained on these points.
in writing. the european parliament voted today on an own-initiative report on the impact of the crisis on access to care for vulnerable groups in europe. there are several points in the report which i cannot support. this includes a long list of oversteps into the member states' decision-making area, and several points that dismiss the difficult but necessary austerity policy of several member states. however, access to care for the most vulnerable groups in society, not least the undocumented, is such an important issue that i chose to vote in favour of the report, despite the major shortcomings mentioned above.

the questioner. - mr president! - what is it? thank you for being here, mrs reding. i would like to say that this is an oral question from the whole committee. a eurobarometer survey shows that almost 70% of europeans see the gender pay gap as a serious problem. today, the pay gap is about 16 percent. this obviously affects women throughout their lives. if you go into a store, you don't get a 16 percent discount. if you have to pay rent or interest on the house, you don't get a 16 percent discount. if you're going to go by car or bus, you don't get a 16 percent discount on gas or bus fares. as far as the benefits are concerned, you have 16 percent lower benefits in the health insurance systems and in the pension system you unfortunately get 16 percent less in the end. there are two reasons for this. on the one hand, there is of course a pure wage discrimination, but there is also something that is structurally discriminatory in terms of pay in the gender segregated labour market, where women's work is valued less than men's work. for example, in a grocery store, a woman working in the checkout area may be paid less than a man working on the floor lifting goods. or a midwife in france who is paid less than her equal colleagues despite having the same job or the same education. in may 2012, the gender equality committee asked the commission to review the existing directive as it appears that not much has changed in the 40 years of this directive. i know that viviane reding and the commission share the concern of the european parliament about today's pay gap. however, the commission has not yet responded to the gender equality committee's request. so far the commission has said that it is not going to do anything about this because it is waiting for an investigation that is not yet complete. but the commission has promised to return to the european parliament in the first half of 2013. now it's september and we're still waiting. the question you have to ask yourself, though, is: how long will european women have to wait? how long are we going to tolerate this discrimination? how long are we going to tolerate violations of fundamental rights? if we all agree that the gender pay gap is a serious problem, why are we doing nothing? while the member states are undoubtedly the main responsible for the current wage discrimination, the commission has a specific responsibility as it is the body empowered to make new proposals. the pay gap is, of course, primarily about power structures in our society. women are degraded for the simple reason that they are women. we therefore need policy reforms aimed at eliminating wage discrimination and creating a much more integrated and equal labour market. an important prerequisite here is the principle of collective agreements, which the committee on gender equality has also taken a position on. tools are needed, such as gender equality authorities to monitor, an obligation for companies to report pay transparently and a comprehensive legislation with the possibility of penalties. we need more transparency. wages are and should not be a private matter. legal aid must be offered to those who are discriminated against by an employer. we must not forget that equal pay for equal work is one of the founding principles of the european union. it's illegal to discriminate against women in the labor market, yet very little is done. my committee stresses the importance of working aggressively to reduce the wage gap. the question is what the commission intends to do, because almost 70% of europeans see the gender pay gap as a very serious problem.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the gender pay gap starts at the very first job. the gaps increase during the rest of working life. not only are men's wages higher, but men also have a greater wage spread, which leads to better earnings growth throughout their working lives and significantly higher pensions. is that fair? the eu has violated both eu treaties, directives and un conventions on equal pay and equality before the law for almost 50 years. that we're not ashamed! when will there be a change? we're just gonna wait another 50 years? it takes a lot to achieve a society where everyone benefits. both the eu and the member states have a responsibility in this respect. when will we stop discriminating against women? what measures and steps does the commission intend to take?
mr president, i would like to thank you. i am pleased that a first trialogue agreement on the market abuse regulation is now on the table. the legislation we are about to adopt is, in my view, a major step forward in several respects. an important aspect is that the scope is greatly extended. the new rules cover the manipulation of key interest rates such as libor and euribor. the same applies to insider dealing and market manipulation in the newspaper commodity trading, emission allowance trading and otc trading outside the regular market places. this creates a regulatory framework covering a very large part of the market. the white spots on the map are being reduced considerably. i think that's great. another important aspect is that whistleblowers - i.e. internal whistleblowers - are given better protection than before. the employees of different financial companies should be able to report misconduct anonymously without fear of reprisals from their employers. this is a crucial improvement which will definitely result in a fairer and more stable market. the negotiations on market abuse must now be pushed forward so that the related directives are also put in place.
for the alde group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. this is an important report because it contains a safeguard clause for the temporary suspension of visa liberalisation for third countries when one of our member states is faced with an increase in the number of unfounded asylum applications. but the goal - which i want to emphasise - is that we should achieve visa liberalisation after all. through the reciprocity mechanism we are increasing solidarity between our countries and at the same time sending a very strong signal to the outside world, namely that all member states should be treated equally when it comes to visas. as regards the committee procedure, the alde group recommends that we vote this week and not use the debate on delegated acts as a reason for rejecting the agreement which, after much effort and difficulty, has nonetheless been reached. what i want to stress today is that we need more solidarity. we need to show that solidarity and show that we belong together to the outside world.
in writing. i have chosen to abstain from voting. of course, the importance of energy efficiency should be emphasised, energy efficiency in, among other things, buildings is a key part of becoming a more sustainable society. the report also stresses the need for financial instruments that will go directly to private enterprise and public-private partnerships.
mr president, i would like to thank you. commissioner, please! you guys! the european parliament, like the commission, is keen to reach a decision on the amending budget2 in the context of the agreement on the next long-term budget. why, you may ask. payment appropriations for 2013 - what do they have to do with the future? well, it has to do with the future in more ways than one. firstly, it is about the credibility of eu policy. the eu must emerge from its payments crisis if we are to maintain confidence in our investments in, among other things, research and education. what kind of scientist enters a european research project if he is not sure that he will be paid in the end? who sends students on erasmus exchanges if they are not sure that the eu will cover their share of the cost? at the moment, the eu cannot pay its bills. secondly: the increasingly politicized dispute over the payment appropriations which should be a technical consequence of the eu's budgetary ambitions must be brought to an end. it would be devastating if, in addition to the sharply reduced long-term budget, which reduces the eu's ambitions, the union were to have difficulties in implementing even these hard-won programmes because of insufficient payment appropriations. thirdly: the eu budget is not allowed to run a deficit under the treaty. if we were nevertheless to drag a lot of unpaid bills into the next budget period, this could be the same threat to credibility as when some euro countries got away with their breaches of the stability and growth pact. we are pleased that a large majority of member states - unfortunately with some exceptions - are taking their responsibilities seriously and we look forward to a serious consideration of the second part of this amending budget which the commission is expected to present shortly.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. cybersecurity is a civilian issue and it's important that we focus on the civilian side and build resilience. an example from my country is that there was a flood in a basement, after which train traffic was shut down for three days because the control systems were down. it is not criminals or other governments that will cause most of the disruption. it's accidents and incompetence. that's what we have to plan for. we can't save money and shut down critical systems at the slightest disturbance. i hope that's what our cybersecurity work will focus on. the commissioner was right when he said that europeans do not want to be monitored. however, the reality is quite different here and we must - both the commission and ourselves - take legislative initiatives so that cybersecurity also means security from being monitored, security to safeguard our privacy. with those conditions, we all have success and can succeed. finally, we also need increased responsibilities for software vendors to deal with security vulnerabilities. it's not like you always have to click "accept the terms" and they can always get away with all the responsibility for bad programming. it's not a strong consumer protection.
mr president, thanks to the rapporteur. it is all very simple. if we want to be the leading global economy, we need to be leading regarding the digital agenda. if we want to regain our leadership, we need to secure that the nokias can buy the microsofts and that the new googles, the new yahoos and the new apples emerge in europe. that is how simple it is. but the difficult thing is that we now need to do the reforms that are needed for that. we need to secure that we have the highest and the best capacities for broadband and internet, because is the pre-condition in order to secure that european citizens can have the best opportunities and we can develop the best services here. we also need to be able to ensure fourth generation take-up, where member states need to implement decisions on the 800 megahertz band, and we need to secure that we can get the transnational investment making the european union the most attractive part of the global economy regarding investment. we need to allocate the frequencies needed to have the mobile data traffic that a leading global economy needs to have. that means the 700 megahertz band, which this parliament has already supported, but that also means to secure that we in this part of the world can have all the new leading services. it is very simple, madam commissioner, mr minister: we need to do the difficult things and if we do not to do the difficult things, we will not regain the leadership that we would have.
mr president, i would like to thank you. we all agree that the digital agenda is something that europe could use a lot of in terms of getting the economy moving and so on. we've seen great hopes for cloud services and so on, but unfortunately, there's been a very big problem in the last week and the last few days with the revelations about what the us spy agency nsa is doing. first of all, we now realize that if a company or an individual puts their data in the cloud, yes, it ends up in the united states. and if the computers are in the united states, then the u.s. intelligence community has full access to all the data and saves and stores it. but it's worse than that. we've also learned that the united states is deliberately sabotaging our infrastructure. they've deliberately built in weaknesses to make the encryption easier to break. they do this to spy on us themselves, but because these back doors exist, terrorists and crooks can also exploit them. we need to make sure we evaluate what's happened and try to save our internet.
mr president, the digital single market is a good objective. copyright reform seems inevitable for this goal, but unfortunately the commission has not even managed an evaluation of the directive from 2001. i would be delighted to have an explanation as to why that is not happening. at the same time, one wonders whether overhauling all of the existing telecommunications legislation without proper evaluation would be the right way forward. we have many urgent problems that are well assessed. one of them is ‘notice and action’: how to ensure a due process for protecting freedom of speech online. but here the commission has so far been unwilling to let parliament have a say. parliament should clarify a clearly dangerous legal uncertainty which the commission has itself established. young people in europe do not trust in the european union and they do not vote in the elections. and why should they? the digital single market needs to be founded on respect for fundamental rights, legal certainty, trust, privacy and resilience. i would be very happy to work with the commission to achieve these goals and to hear the commission’s comments on how to ensure they are achieved.
rapporteur. − mr president, let me first read out directly from the latest scientific advice on eels from the international council for the exploration of the seas (ices) that says: ‘the eel stock remained in a critical state in 2012. the recruitment index ... is currently at its historical lowest, less than 1% for the north sea’. outside the north sea less than 6.5%, compared to the years 1960 to 1979’. ices also says: ‘urgent action is needed. ... all anthropogenic mortality (recreational and commercial fishing, hydropower, pollution) affecting ... eels should be reduced to as close to zero as possible’. if this had been the case for a more charismatic species, let us say swordfish or bluefin tuna, i think the commissioner would have been here in the first place, and i am quite certain that we would now have a very noisy public debate over the lack of urgent action taken; but unfortunately this is the european eel with its fascinating life cycle. as you know it is born and then dies in the sargasso sea and goes through several transformations from glass eel to yellow eel to silver eel. it does not get the public attention it deserves. the regulation that is now before you, and on which we will vote tomorrow, dates back to 2007 when member states, after many years of discussion, finally agreed to do something about the eel decline. the debate has been going on for more than 10 years now and the regulation has been in place for more than five years, and during all this time the eel stock has continued to decline. in 2012 the advice from ices sounded yet another alarm bell. the restocking of eels that has been allowed as a conservation measure is – and i quote again – ‘unlikely to contribute to the recovery of the european eel population in a substantial manner’. this is indeed a huge problem since many of the member states’ eel recovery plans rely heavy on eel restocking that is now conducted without any guidelines or criteria at all regarding survival of glass eels, or conditions to allow silver eels to escape to the sargasso sea to spawn. basically what we are adding now to the old regulations are two very important things. first of all, that the commission should present a report on the effectiveness of restocking as a conservation measure. secondly, we are asking the commission to come up with a new legislative proposal before the end of march next year that will, with high probability, save the european eel. i hope too that the council will see the need for this and that the council will not try and bury this regulation along with all the other long-term management plans, because of the different interpretations of allocation of fishing opportunities. there are no allocations of fishing opportunities in this regulation, so after the vote in parliament i look forward to a speedy process forward. a lot of things are at stake here, not only the survival of the eel but also the credibility of the eu as a serious player on the international arena, promoting sustainable fisheries and even imposing trade sanctions on third countries that allow unsustainable fisheries. remember that the european eel is listed as an acutely endangered species and that it falls under the cites convention on trade in endangered species. i want to thank all colleagues and shadow rapporteurs that have supported this report. to those that think we should stick to simple ‘lisbonisation’ in this process i want to say that i am quite certain that future generations will not understand the logic of doing a simple legal adjustment to this regulation which has the fate of an entire species in its hands.
the s&d group is in favour of the proposal. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the european eel stock is in a terrible state. it is about fishing and the dramatic changes in the life-cycle of a fish that not only has a complex life cycle but can live for decades before the next generation is born. we must therefore take all reasonable steps to ensure that we create the right conditions for the recovery of the eel. a mix of rapid measures in line with the latest scientific assessments of available stocks, but also long-term measures to restore migration routes and create long-term conditions will be required. we have not wanted to advocate a total cessation of fishing, a measure which, of course, sounds very forceful, but which would undermine the incentive for voluntary action and perhaps even be harmful, since it would eliminate the possibilities of discarding. i would rather see a requirement that when eel are released it should be done in the most responsible and efficient way possible, to ensure that this is good for the stock. we have also called on the commission to come forward with a new plan based on the latest scientific reports. i hope that this will mean a safer future for the european eel, but then all the member states will have to contribute in a better way than has been done in the past.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would first like to thank isabella lövin for her fantastic and very important work. there are many myths about the eel. in the past, you didn't even know where it came from. no one had ever seen an eel born. for example, it was thought to have originated from sun-heated dew or from mud. today we know that the eel has a very, very complicated life cycle. it is born and dies in the sargasso sea, far from european waters, and it has been found impossible to breed an eel in captivity at all. if we want the eel to remain in europe, whether we want to protect it or eat it, we need international cooperation. i am therefore very pleased with this report by mrs isabella lövin, which provides us with a basis for this. but if we really want to see a difference, it's really about banning eel fishing until the stock recovers. there is no sign that recovery could happen without drastic measures. so the question is when do we dare to make such a decision, otherwise there is a very real risk that there will be no eel left at all. as isabella lövin said, how do we explain to future generations that we exterminated the eel?
member of the commission. − mr president, i would like to repeat that the commission proposal aims at the ‘lisbonisation’ of our legislation: what we are simply trying to do is align the existing regulation to the new treaty – the treaty of lisbon. we are not interfering with the substance. still, i understand that it is reasonable, since we have so many indications that the stock is not doing well, for members of parliament to express their concerns, and i would like to reiterate that the commission is going to take on board your concerns. what we have already done is that we have asked our scientists to give us an assessment about the management plans by the member states, about the reporting system and about the other aspects you have already mentioned. so we are waiting for this assessment; we are then going to analyse it and i can promise you again that by the end of the year the commission will come back to you, to parliament and to the council, with a report about an assessment of what we have been doing until now and what is needed to improve the situation of the stock.
rapporteur. − mr president, i would like to thank my colleagues very much for participating in this debate and for supporting the report. i thank those colleagues who mentioned all the other aspects that are threatening eels stocks: there is no debate about that at all. however, i must emphasise to the members from northern ireland that using the word ‘sustainables’ in relation to eel fisheries today is very difficult because we have a single european eel stock. even though some fishermen might see themselves as being sustainable, when we have a 99% decline in the eel stock and we do not have any criteria for or monitoring of the conditions of restocking, it is very difficult to call a fishery sustainable today. what we are asking for, commissioner, includes an evaluation of the restocking measures, and that is actually something new. the international council for the exploration of the sea (ices) has expressed great concern over restocking and whether it actually contributes to the eel stock recovery or not. there is concern about the mortality during fishing of small glass eels, the possible transfer of diseases during transport, and whether or not stocked eels can actually survive and swim to the sargasso sea to spawn, rather than simply get fished. i would say to the epp group that i respect your wish to stick to the alignment. however, it is sometimes difficult to be consistent even inside your own group. i know that, with regard to the cod management plan in the baltic, the epp group was actually in favour of some very substantial changes to the ‘lisbonisation’ report, so you are not always very consistent in your approach. i would like to thank the commissioner for understanding that the urgency of this situation actually calls for some additional elements in the eel recovery plan. to those of you who want to look at the illegal export of glass eels, i would emphasise that i want to do that too, but somehow we are not very consistent in the european union. on the one hand, we acknowledge that the european eel is acutely endangered and should not be exported at all and, at the same time, we are very hesitant about taking action with regard to the trade inside the union. we have to be consistent, especially at a time when the eu is now considering trade sanctions against other countries that fish unsustainably. we have to live up to the same criteria ourselves.
rapporteur. − mr president, this report is about creating jobs and growth in europe without spending taxpayers’ money. the services directive has been on our lips now since it was adopted in 2006 but, i beg my colleagues, let us not give in to services directive fatigue. the question still is: how many jobs are we losing on a daily basis because the internal market for services is not fully functioning? market completion is the highway out of the crisis. with the service sector accounting for nearly 70% of eu gdp and total employment, it is a pillar of our economy. progress has been made, but much too slowly and there are still unjustified restrictions and barriers which hamper the movement of services. is it, for instance, normal that nationality requirements and regional authorisations are still in place in some parts of the eu? is it proportionate to impose a change of business model if an architect’s practice from one member state wants to establish its company in another member state? is it acceptable to impose higher prices on non-residents for rescue packages or for travel? should you, mr president, need to have another insurance company when you already have a contract with an insurance company in your member state? these are unacceptable costs for small businesses. i welcome the great job that the commission has done and in particular the personal commitment of commissioner barnier – welcome, commissioner – to the policy of non-tolerance. the commission has identified breaches and is going to report to the council in october on loophole restrictions which have been identified, striving to maximise the economic effects. there are still too many double standards. rules exist, but they are not properly enforced and i welcome the infringement procedures launched by the commission. i do not see these as punitive measures. we must also engage in dialogue with member states, and the irish presidency and now the lithuanian presidency have both cooperated excellently with us in putting this on the agenda in the competitiveness council. i would also call on national parliaments and all the stakeholders to play their role in putting pressure on member states. some member states are not fully implementing the services directive in good faith, but that is no excuse. they are still accountable for depriving citizens of jobs. we have to remove these community barriers because it will not cost us to do so; it is just an effective way to generate growth. in particular, we have to look at the grey zones – the hidden fragmentation and the uncertainty of rules – which create confusion and delays for service providers. overriding reasons of public interest are invoked by national authorities in a way that is exaggerated. too seldom are proportionality assessments carried out – and i do suggest that the commission clarifies and provides guidance to member states on how to apply the proportionality test. likewise, i think we have to have a country-specific recommendation in the european semester, in the peer review and also linking to the implementation and full enforcement of the rules of the services directive. the economic needs tests have to be lifted, and unjustified requirements that shut down operators from other member states have to be removed. the internal market information system (imi) and solvit and the points of single contact have to be implemented fully in a way that is accessible throughout the eu and universally known. most importantly, mr president – and i will finish with this – implementation of the services directive should be put on the european council agenda and kept there for as long as it takes.
member of the commission. - madam president, i will be brief, but i would like to thank all the members who have expressed their views clearly and frankly, but who are all joining in asking the commission to implement this directive on services that you have adopted, just this directive and all this directive, with intelligence and pragmatism, in consultation with the member states. that's our job, and that's what i intend to do. mr morganti, with regard to the beaches, which have often been brought to my attention, that is exactly what i am obliged to do. these beach concessions are covered by the directive. the european parliament has voted on the text as it stands and i am responsible for implementing it. i am trying to do this as intelligently as possible and i am continuing my consultations, in a constructive way, to apply it on the spot - without calling it into question but, on the contrary, by strengthening tourist activity - with the italian authorities. mr ferreira recalled the concerns related to this services directive, in particular the risk of weakening the services previously provided by the state. i would remind you that this directive, to which you contributed so much a few years ago, is a directive on administrative simplification. social services are partly covered, and parliament had rejected an amendment which proposed their exclusion. if they are provided by the state, they are nevertheless excluded from the scope of this directive. as regards economic services of general interest, the provisions of the directive on temporary crossborder provision do not apply. many of you, and especially mrs gebhardt, who has worked a lot on this subject, have mentioned the social dimension, the protection of workers. i would like to reiterate that the directive as i read it, as i understand it and as i want to apply it, is very clear on this point: it does not affect labour law, nor the right to negotiate, conclude or apply collective agreements, nor the right to take collective action. i am quite honestly not aware of any member state which has now gone beyond this directive in terms of labour law. the national parliaments must play an important role here, alongside you, in ensuring that the measures taken at national level to implement this directive on services safeguard social rights. finally, madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to take from the very thorough and very intentional report by mrs corazza bildt a main message - addressed not only to me but also to the member states - to complete the implementation of this directive, to make it achieve its full potential. we know that the service economy in the european union has real growth potential, although i think that europe should not be content with being a service economy and that it should regain its manufacturing ambition in industry, but that is another matter. on the other hand, as regards the question of service communities - which we know are important in europe - we must complete the implementation of this directive. the way in which i will continue to implement it, in liaison with the member states, with intransigence, with zero tolerance, will be clearly written - i say it again publicly, have no doubt about it - in the economic line which is mine, that of the european project from the beginning - a line which should never have been abandoned even if this has sometimes been the case, as i have seen in the field of financial markets - the line of the competitive social market economy. economy, social, market, competitive: these four words, not just one, two or three of them, are important to me.
rapporteur. − madam president, i truly appreciate all my colleagues’ comments and i do understand the various sensitivities and the need to take into account the situation in the member states. the best way to include the social dimension is to make sure that people have a job. the best way to stand up for social rights is to make sure that people can, with a job, take care of their families and of their children. as ms gebhardt so rightly said, the services directive fully includes, in article 1, the social dimension and social rights. the treaty of lisbon does not need to be repeated. we all stand for it and have to respect it. there are two positions here. one is pragmatic, the one that commissioner barnier has been driving so fantastically well. that is to say: let us look at the restrictions, let us look at the loopholes, let us look at the problems. let us see how concretely we can work with member states to make sure that we remove the obstacles that deprive citizens of jobs, growth and competitiveness. and then you have an ideological position which says: let us repeat a thousand times what is already in the text, just to make the point that we stand for social rights. i would like to call on all of you, colleagues, to assume your responsibilities. and that includes our friends! it is true, ms rapti, that we have worked very closely together and i am truly surprised that, at the last minute in plenary, after we had gone the extra mile to make all the compromises that were satisfactory to the red and green groups, you should come here with threats and red lines. i do not understand this position. you have to be accountable to the people of europe, not to me. i call on you all, colleagues, to join forces and to focus the message to the member states to keep this issue at the highest level on the political agenda in the european union, removing obstacles in the interests of the full functioning of the internal market for services and in the interests of competitiveness, jobs and growth. please do not dilute the message. let us vote in a united way tomorrow, to implement fully the internal market for services.
mr president, i would like to thank the president of the commission for his presentation of the state of the union. i think we have two challenges. one is to deal with the consequences of the problems of the crisis which, as he underlined, we still have to fight. at the same time we also need to meet the challenges we have seen emerging, because the global economy in 2013 is not the same as before we entered the crisis in 2008. we face new and rapidly emerging competition in all areas and it is not enough to deal with the problems of the crisis. we must also accelerate the reforms needed to keep up with and to take up the global competition. i think one way of describing the change is that this year there will be more mobile-phone connections than human beings, which is a sign of the level of prosperity. it is also the sign of a totally new global economy. it is not enough to be as good as we were in 2008. we must be much better and that requires reforms in all areas. i would like the commission, as well as the presidency of the council, to see the challenges that we have in the global economy. that underlines for me that we must now take action to make reforms to the european economy for the internal market and ensure that we have full freedom of movement in all areas, not least the service sector. regarding the telecoms sector, we must be insistent and decisive in achieving a european telecoms market, which is one of the most important things in order to enable the whole of the european economy. i said yesterday, and i say now, that we need to ensure that we in europe can achieve the new googles, the new yahoos and the new apples. we must also ensure that in the future nokias will buy microsofts and not the other way round. i would like us to make the reforms that will turn europe into the leading economy. that requires difficult decisions and we are all responsible for taking them.

rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on fisheries. − mr president, i want to thank sandrine bélier and also my colleague ian hudghton whose opinion was adopted with a complete majority in the committee on fisheries– a rare occurrence for the fisheries committee. all of the rapporteur’s amendments were subsequently adopted by the lead committee – and such an agreement between the fisheries committee and the environment committee is rarer still. mr hudghton’s opinion deals with two very important issues: first of all it makes express reference to the need to involve indigenous and local communities in relation to access – something which is in the protocol, but missing from the commission’s proposal – and, secondly, it recognises the possibility for the eu to extend the protocol’s scope to activities taking place in international waters beyond the economic zone, outside the 200 nautical miles limit – and this is extremely important because two thirds of the world’s oceans, one half of the globe, is actually outside national jurisdiction.
