in writing. - we voted against the own-initiative report on the european cultural and creative sectors as sources of economic growth and employment because we strongly oppose the exclusion of audiovisual and cultural content services from eu-third country agreements, and in particular from the negotiating mandate for the eu-us free trade agreement. such an exemption hinders economic growth in the cultural and creative sectors and is counterproductive to transatlantic cooperation. we would like to emphasize that we also voted against increased public support measures and the establishment of a cultural investment bank.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the commission put forward a proposal which was too restrictive in terms of health claims. the council corrected it and made it much more difficult. however, the member states were never given the opportunity to say no to this. in fact, i think there's a majority of us who think it's kind of strange that you would put on a pasta package that carbohydrates help maintain normal brain function. i wonder if there is anyone in europe today who suffers from carbohydrate deficiency. no, i don't think that's what you mean. even if efsa has investigated and said that it is true, i think that the commission, parliament and the council of ministers should have taken their responsibility and said that it is true, but it is a meaningless claim that should not be allowed to exist. now, unfortunately, all we have to do is choose between what's on the table and saying no. if we say no, however, it would mean that the claims would be on dextrostablet, on sugar - everywhere they would be. i would personally like to say no to this, to turn around and get a total ban, but like the social democrats, the green group argues that this is a risky strategy, because we do not know when such a process would be completed. then these claims could be on even more absurd products than pasta. but we should actually do something about the fact that a majority of us here and a majority of the population and certainly a majority of the governments, if they were asked, think we shouldn't have ridiculous claims about food. it lowers the credibility of the health claims that are actually relevant, because we want people to eat more of something that's healthy. but like i said, no european is suffering from carbohydrate deficiency.
for the alde group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. it is very easy to understand that there is a fear that we will lose food security. i think, however, that in this case we are allowed to think. as the previous speaker said, it is a little dangerous to pick out a piece of a whole, because food control of animal products should start with the inspection of the live animal. it must be carried out and be extremely thorough. it's where we can see how the animal is doing and how the animal has been treated and lived. i'm not a big believer in the old-fashioned methods of examining the carcass. i think that an ocular examination is probably quite acceptable, but that we should be much more careful than we are now in the microbiological examination of carcasses, because the zoonoses with which we have the greatest problem in europe today are not actually visible, with or without a knife in hand. we need a microscope if we're going to control them. i think that the commission's proposals are partly good and partly not good. i would very much like to see this debate taken up and put into a broader context.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. it is not appropriate to accept proposals which save the slaughter industry money but not those which cost money. you can't pick and choose certain pieces that benefit industry. true, the kind of problems that are found by palpation have become rarer. but even if only 1.3% of carcasses are infected, and even if the detection rate were only 0.3% different from today, that still means that 400,000 europeans would have a piece of meat on their plate every day that has blisters or tumors. although it is claimed that this would be found further down the slaughter chain, we have experience that if unserious operators - if this is not stopped already at the slaughterhouse - found unsuitable pieces of meat somewhere in the chain, they would come back. if we put them in the slaughterhouse, there's fewer actors, more chance of stopping it early enough to not end up on our plates. that's reason enough to say no to this. moreover, animal welfare organisations and veterinarians have also said that it is not appropriate to do this without also taking other measures that were proposed, but which the commission now does not want to do. finally, there may be a risk that small slaughterhouses or small farmers, that is to say small businesses, will be affected more than large ones, as marit paulsen very rightly said, that we should have the whole animal welfare chain intact. we need to find a solution to that problem, too.
for the verts/ale group. i am pleased to see that the commission has taken this step. we have now finally reached a new common fisheries policy. one of the most important principles of the external fisheries policy is that the eu negotiates only for a surplus of fish that the coastal nation itself does not need, and that the local population does not need. as regards this agreement with mauritania, mauritanian fishermen themselves have a market and a need for the squid. the fact that our rapporteur recommends that we reject this agreement because spanish fishermen want this squid is immoral in my view. we must not only comply with our new fisheries policy, but also with the convention on the law of the sea. article 62 stipulates that no country may claim any fish that the coastal country itself needs. mauritania is a developing country, and we must respect and give it the opportunity to develop itself. that is why i support this agreement. i think it is an exemplary agreement of which the eu should be proud.
for the verts/ale group. president. - the debate is closed. tobacco kills four times as many europeans each year as workplace accidents, traffic accidents, homicides, aids, and all other drugs combined. the measures we are now proposing, i.e. tightening up the rules, are in principle free of charge for society, but the health benefits could be even greater. attitudes towards tobacco can be changed so that the next generation of europeans does not have to grow up in the womb exposed to the harmful effects of tobacco, and so that we do not have a situation where 70 per cent of smokers start as children, before the age of 18. so we are no longer talking about free choice, but we are talking about our democratic responsibility to help children not to choose the path of tobacco and suffering. in view of this, i find it scandalous that people in this house, particularly from the ppe group, in their amendments, take the line of the tobacco industry on all the issues that the tobacco industry has identified as important, even though your negotiator has had a very good position and good cooperation with the rest of us. why is the warning text at the top? yes, because it is to warn people! then it should be seen and not hidden behind a shelf edge. there's no objective reason to keep it at the bottom. the only purpose is to hide it. i am in favour of this variant, as in australia, where the whole package is avoided as the last marketing ploy in the tobacco companies' arsenal. i know i will lose, but then at least we can avoid undermining the commission's proposal and retain 75%. delegated acts sound like a technical detail, but if we delete all of them, we make it very difficult for the commission to adapt the directive to reality when new problems arise. it is important that we do not delete delegated acts. it is another priority of the tobacco industry. i hope that these proposals will be voted down. the big question is also the next generation of products. we have now focused on article 18 on e-cigarettes. we're talking about a product that got 160,000 new american teens started using nicotine last year. it's not just a weaning product, it's also a weaning product. the tobacco industry itself expects to make more money from this in ten years than from cigarette sales. if you have a nicotine cigarette with a piña colada flavor, there is a high chance that someone will accidentally pour it into their drink mix and actually die of an overdose. we must regulate this in a serious way, so that we avoid damage. finally, the commission the taste of the snus will be decided by sweden, and licorice pipes will not be banned.
mr president, i would like to thank you. smoking kills - we all know that. in fact, much has been done to prevent smoking both here in the eu and in our own countries. today, we take further necessary steps. the most important thing, as has been pointed out here, is that children and young people do not start smoking. commissioner, we swedes must be allowed to keep our snus. it's part of our culture. sweden has had a legal exemption for snus since our membership in 1995. banning snus in sweden would be like banning alsacevin here in strasbourg. my message is clear: don't touch that snot! all legislation must be reasonable and proportionate. banning licorice pipes is actually a bunch of crap. my message is clear: don't touch the licorice pipes!
in writing. - we voted today in favour of two reports on the use of the european globalisation adjustment fund. since the fund was set up in 2006, we have always abstained from voting, because we were against the fund from the beginning, as we do not believe that the fund is the right instrument to help workers affected by structural changes. the fund has now grown, evolved and received its own allocation. it exists and will not disappear for the foreseeable future. we have therefore now decided to take a case-by-case view of whether it meets the very strict requirements for co-financing and long-term employment measures imposed on applicants. in the above case, we considered that the applications fulfilled these requirements. we would like to emphasize that we are opposed to the reintroduction of the possibility of using the fund for other purposes than its original purpose. we will continue to support more effective and long-term instruments in the framework of budgetary and long-term budgetary work to create more jobs, respond to structural change and increase labour market flexibility.
in writing. - i voted in favour of this report. i am doing this in order to provide workers in these cases with financial support for retraining and/or other measures to find new jobs. however, i would like to stress that the main problem with regard to the high level of unemployment in the eu is the austerity policy advocated by the commission and decided upon by the governments of the member states. to create new jobs in europe, a new economic policy with massive public and environmentally friendly investments is needed. investments for the benefit of society are needed in education, health and care, but also in infrastructure, railways, public transport, energy-efficient housing and sustainable energy systems.

for the gue/ngl group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. one in three women worldwide is a victim of physical or sexual violence. the problem is shockingly common in both rich and poor countries. violence against women is a widespread social problem that also affects the economy. violence against women is rooted in the gendered order in which we live. violence against women is a violation of human rights. that is why the istanbul convention is so good, because it clarifies this very well. the convention takes a holistic approach to the fight against violence, focusing on prevention, protection and support, prosecution and integrated policies. the main focus is on women and girls, but other victims of violence in close relationships are also included, such as men, children and the elderly. i therefore commend the istanbul convention for its dual nature, being both a human rights convention and a criminal law convention. the european parliament has repeatedly called on the commission to present a directive on violence against women. the commission has so far refused to put forward a proposal covering all forms of violence against women. as chairman of the femm committee, i have often asked the council for help in changing the commission's mind. but i understand that the council is not interested in introducing a directive at eu level either. but then you should really be interested in the member states signing this intergovernmental convention. now, the chairman of the board and the representatives don't hear what i'm saying, but i'm gonna say this: you want to fight violence against women and girls, as we do, right? so my question to the council is: what does the council intend to do to get more countries to ratify and sign this convention? what does the lithuanian presidency intend to do to bring this issue up on the agenda?
mr president, domestic violence is not a private issue. it is a serious crime and a global phenomenon. we need to keep violence against women – from honour killings to sexual abuse and all other forms of violence – at the top of our list of priorities. that is why i have been joining and supporting the initiatives of colleagues in this parliament to ensure that member states meet their responsibilities to sign and ratify the council of europe istanbul convention. we have also adopted concrete instruments in this parliament: the protection of victims, the european protection order, and the mutual recognition of protection measures in civil matters. these are really helpful to protect more women and to ensure that perpetrators are brought to justice. they need to be implemented and i call on the member states to fully and correctly enforce them and respect the deadlines. as many of you have said, prevention is also very important, including raising awareness and education. we need to do more together on gender stereotypes, on stigmatisation, on discrimination, to empower women and to promote equality. it is about changing the culture, developing a culture of respect for the rights of women and girls. too many women and children continue to live in fear and uncertainty and the time has come to help them break the silence, break the isolation, break the loneliness. we have the responsibility to make sure that victims – be they men, children, women or girls – feel confident enough to speak up and be heard, and to trust that they will be protected. i will continue to be committed to zero tolerance for impunity and to encourage member states and the commission to come up with a comprehensive and clear strategy for the eradication of all violence against women and children.
mr president, i would like to thank you. china is the world's third largest economy and the eu's second largest trading partner after the united states. the eu, on the other hand, is china's largest trading partner. therefore, a bilateral investment agreement between the eu and china, focusing on better market access, is something that would bring these countries closer together, and could lead to huge economic gains for all involved, not least in terms of jobs and growth. i am therefore very pleased that the commission has presented this communication and that we in the european parliament are now going to adopt this resolution tomorrow. i know - and we hear this in the debate - that many colleagues are deeply concerned about such an agreement. they worry about human rights, environmental impact, social impact, but most of all they worry about chinese companies competing with european companies and jobs. i don't think we need to have that much confidence. european companies are successful and have opportunities in china. however, we cannot ignore the fact that china has human rights and environmental impact problems. then an investment agreement is not a threat but part of the solution. if we only see threats everywhere, we'll miss all opportunities. an investment agreement opens up exactly those opportunities. that is why i am also saddened to see that the resolution we are now about to vote on is almost more negative than positive. you get the feeling we don't want to have this deal. i think that the resolution's attempt to exclude a whole range of areas already is counterproductive. last but not least, i think that the european parliament is acting in a contradictory way: to accuse china of using state subsidies is right, but to say in the same breath that we should have a lot of subsidies is not reasonable. then we'll speak with tongues.

exist. the small island is only a boisterous outpost province on a temporary excursion. in this way, taiwan has effectively been frozen out of the world community and denied membership in everything from the un to the who. this is something europe should not be playing with, and i don't think we need to. new zealand has managed to conclude a free trade agreement, japan has negotiated an investment agreement. taiwan we must trust taiwan would never dare or want to make a deal with europe if it jeopardised relations with china. in addition, it puts taiwan in a very difficult position when we conclude agreements with its main competitors. the competitors with whom we now have agreements produce almost exactly the same things, but they now have free access to europe. in practice, we are depriving taiwan of access to the world's largest market, europe. we are leaving the taiwanese out in the cold, all because we have more regard for a big and strong dictatorship than a small and threatened democracy. while free trade and economic gains are the main arguments in favour of this, it should not be the determining factor in the economy or security policy. i think it's a moral issue. therefore, for the sake of both taiwan and europe, we should consider starting negotiations on an ambitious free trade agreement as a matter of urgency. the biggest obstacle we have to overcome to get there, i think, is our own fear and cowardice.

for the alde group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. two weeks ago sweden was shocked by the appalling news that the police had for a number of years kept a register of people of roma origin. the scope is not yet fully established, but it is beyond doubt that it contains several thousand names, and hundreds of them are children, some newborn or a few months old. the revelation has sent shockwaves through sweden and the entire eu. sweden has so far been regarded as a model of independent government and transparency. what has now emerged is that we are facing a major legal scandal. there is no support in swedish legislation for establishing registers based on ethnicity. it is also possible to fear that it violates the eu charter of fundamental rights on several points. there's an investigation going on in sweden. i have asked the commission to look at this very carefully too. once again i stress the importance of the commission actually investigating what has happened. both at national level in sweden and in the eu, we are working on roma integration strategies. all of this falls flat on its face when the police, the very heart of every rule of law, stigmatizes in this way. the stigma varies from country to country, but what is common is that the systematic persecution is present throughout our union. the statement by the french minister of the interior, mr valls, that roma must be expelled from the country, the expulsion of roma from italy and france, the attacks on roma in hungary are just a few examples of the tragically systematic violations. all member states must now respect fundamental freedoms and rights. i have pointed out shortcomings in many member states. today i am painfully and embarrassingly aware that this also applies to my own country.

in writing. − i abstained on the resolution on the alleged illegal flights and detentions by the cia in european countries because, although it is important that member states seek clarification on and investigate these serious allegations, the recommendations in the report go beyond the principle of subsidiarity. it is up to national authorities and parliaments to investigate and seek clarification on these allegations, in accordance with their own legal systems. investigations are ongoing in the member states concerned and there is no need, in the absence of substantive new developments in the matter, to reiterate direct accusations addressed to individual countries. i believe that the european parliament has an important political role to play in increasing transparency about these sensitive issues and putting pressure on member states to respect fundamental rights and the eu charter of fundamental rights. however, i do not support new eu mechanisms to assess observance of fundamental rights in the member states – effectively making parliament a tribunal of judges.

on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and food safety. − mr president, the procedure started when mayotte had a referendum and then decided to change status. then the commission made the proposal to adapt certain rules to the new status. it chose the correct legal basis based on the different directives’ original legal bases. we have three in environment, one in agriculture and also the coming fishery ones. adaptations were required in all those cases, mostly to give mayotte more time. however, the council approved the correct legal basis and chose a special route in the treaty in 355. that means that it is a council decision, taking away the prerogative of parliament to have the ordinary legislative procedure, thereby undermining what is given to us in the treaty and the power that is conferred to us in the treaty. therefore, we would like to protest against changing the legal basis. we sought advice from the committee on legal affairs and they came to exactly the same conclusion as our legal service, namely that the correct legal basis is what the commission proposed – that it should be a normal legislative procedure. we need to take a quick decision. if we want to follow the normal procedure, i would urge the council to withdraw its legal basis and to accept the commission’s legal basis. then we, as a parliament, could take our responsibility and quickly vote through these measures, because they will need to take effect on 1 january 2014. however, we cannot accept them changing the legal basis and taking the power so that we are only consulted. in that case, we would have to take them to the court. so our recommendation would be to take the council to the court if they do not change the legal basis. we should, therefore, vote try, but no thanks’.

in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'medical device' in the definition of a medical device. people should be able to trust that products on the market are properly tested and safe. patient safety and public health require that the eu regulatory framework reflect the rapid pace of market developments. the current regulatory framework is outdated and has not been renewed since the mid-1990s. this has led to wide differences in national regulatory frameworks between member states and uncertainty about the rules for certain products. in view of this, we support the inclusion of specifically identified high-risk products with an indirect medical function in the medical regulatory framework. although we generally call for caution in broadening the definition of medical devices, we believe that in this case it is justified. for us, the most important thing is to protect the health of our patients. the commission has also proposed that the commission should be given the task of drawing up a report on the implementation of the directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of electronic medical records. however, any prohibition of dangerous substances should be based on a scientific benefit/risk analysis at the level of the authorities. not by political decisions and a zero-risk vision. the consequence of the european parliament's decision will be that dangerous substances will be phased out immediately, unless there are technical or safety constraints that make this impossible. in such cases, the european commission and the european parliament shall decide on the derogation for a maximum of four years. we see major problems with this, both in terms of the functioning of the products, their safety and their cost.

mr president, colleagues, dear commissioner, as the responsible vice-president of the s&d group, i am happy to announce that our group tonight voted – with a large majority – in favour of the position of our shadow rapporteur, guido milana. i want to thank him for his good work. this was particularly important in respect of subsidies for fleet renewal. subsidies for vessel construction, as the commissioner mentioned, were abolished more than ten years ago. to reintroduce them now would be completely contradictory to the fisheries reform, and the s&d group will not support that. if we can avoid such mistakes, we are now close to having a sustainable fisheries reform based on three pillars: a regulation based on sustainability criteria; a fisheries fund used to support fish rather than boats, which is also necessary for future fishermen; and a fisheries agreement with mauritania that should become a model for coming agreements. together this is a major achievement, for the future of which we as a parliament can be truly proud.
mr president, i would like to thank you. as we vote on the eu fisheries fund, it is important to remember the principles of sustainable fisheries that we agreed earlier this year. in february we voted on the rules for fishing and now we are going to vote on the tax money that goes to fisheries. i have a simple principle which i think is important to bear in mind, namely that we must be careful with taxpayers' money. in the long term, the fishing industry must be able to stand on its own feet. that is why the fisheries rules we decided in february are very important. only when stocks are built up will the competitiveness of the fishing industry improve and profitability increase and then the need for subsidies will end in the long term. but until then it is very important that the subsidies we have and the measures and activities we have under the fisheries fund do not maintain or increase the capacity of the european fishing fleet. that is why i am very critical of proposals for new construction, engine replacement, support for young fishermen taking over a vessel, etc. i am afraid that this could risk maintaining fishing capacity and thus affecting the profitability of the fishing industry in the long run. that is why i believe that restraint in voting tomorrow is the best way to ensure that we have fish on our plates and in our seas.
madam president, the electronic link between the amendments to the budget did not work in the system when we prepared the vote today. only having paper copies makes it extremely difficult to find the right amendment corresponding to the right vote in the budget, since they are not in numerical order. in future, i would very much appreciate it if parliament could have two sets of amendments: one in numerical order and one in the current order. otherwise it is almost impossible for anybody not on the committee on budgets quickly to find each vote linked to each amendment.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. politics begins and ends on the streets or, as in this case, on international waters. the detention by russia of peaceful non-violent activists from an international environmental organisation in international waters is a threat to democracy, a threat to freedom of expression and a threat to the right to demonstrate. on monday we discussed in this house the forthcoming cop in poland, and expectations of that meeting seem almost non-existent, perhaps because we have not lived up to the standards that can be expected in international environmental negotiations. that's why we need organizations like greenpeace that continue to act, to shine a light and to fight for our nature and the rights of future generations. the illegal imprisonment of greenpeace activists has caused a lot of international uproar, and the netherlands is a good example of how to act. i think we need to be much more clear about this criticism. it is our democratic duty to demand the release of these greenpeace activists.
on behalf of the ppe group. – mr president, first of all i should like to thank the rapporteur and the commissioner. i think it is striking that the sector we are discussing today is, on the one hand, characterised by extremely complex and slow administrative procedures and, on the other hand, that there are extremely rapid and dynamic developments in it. that means that we need to look ahead to the coming ten years more than we need to look back over the last five or three years. secondly – and this is crucial – we need to fight fragmentation. it is a paradox that, in these varying mobile and global markets, we in the european union have more fragmentation than in nearly any other sector of our economy. i think we need to focus on opportunities for achieving transnational european markets, transnational operators, and the benefits for consumers of having those. i think it is fair to say that here we have the impact assessment of reality – not from any bureaucratic department or division, but we have the impact assessment from reality, because the reality is that europe is lagging behind. it is of crucial importance for us to speed up, because otherwise we will lag behind more and more. the things we do not do today will mean that we lag behind even more tomorrow. that is why we need to ensure that we can deal with difficult spectrum issues, the 700 mhz band, and the transnational perspective. we need to ensure that we in europe can have the highest speeds, not the old-fashioned speeds of the digital agenda. we need to be better than that, and we need to make use of the fact that we now have a recovery. no other area provides such good investment and competitiveness as these telecom sectors. that will help society as a whole. that is what we have a duty to achieve.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to ask the same question as jens rohde: why are these proposals coming now? these were actually issues that we discussed when commissioner kroes was elected as commissioner and we members of parliament asked tough questions about which programme she was going to work on. now, at the last minute of the term, the proposals come. they are also combined with enormous expectations. with the telecommunications market we are going to fight unemployment, we are going to fight the crisis, we are going to tackle youth unemployment, and we are also going to do very good for consumers. i feel that it is like a giant mountain that someone has pushed in front of them until we are at the very end of a term. it may well be that the blame should not be laid on commissioner kroes but on other parts of the commission. in any case, we need to have reasonable expectations and a reasonable view of where we are. there are three different markets within the eu: a northern telecommunications market, a - let me call it - central european market and a southern market. you have to admit they're there. it must be the guiding principle for the proposals which are then tried to be implemented. you don't start from scratch. it requires careful analysis, it requires transparency, it requires thoughtfulness and that is what i lack in this work. however, the ultimate consumer benefit is the most important. i shall be following with open eyes the proposals that are likely to be put forward.
on behalf of the s&d group. – madam president, colleagues, commissioner, mr minister, there was a heated debate in the budget committee this week regarding amending budget no 6. let me, however, emphasise that nobody objected to the contents, which are merely a technical update of the figures for 2013 own resources, bni contributions, rebates, etc. the debate instead concerned the procedure, the very sudden adoption by the council and the equally sudden proposal from the commission that this must be introduced on this week’s agenda. the background is, of course, the commission’s acute cash-flow problem and the inability to pay outstanding bills. this has, unfortunately, been caused not by the commission, as suggested by some conservative members of the budget committee, but by the member states and their constant under-budgeting of payments. amending budget no 6, however, has nothing to do with a lack of payment appropriations. it is only an update of the figures for revenue to be paid by the member states, but it has an effect on cash-flow. to avoid any suspicion that the council in this situation would take the adoption of amending budget no 6 as an argument for delaying and not adopting amending budget no 8, my group has tabled an amendment in order to link the adoption of amending budget no 6 with amending budget no 8. the wording needs a minor adjustment, which i will introduce as an oral amedment at the vote. for this reason, to avoid further misunderstandings, i thank the presidency and especially minister rimkūnas for having made things clear here and for having confirmed that the council will, as one of the remaining conditions, adopt amending budget no 8 in time for this parliament to give its consent to the mff in november, thereby preventing another budget crisis. i hope now that nothing can change that promise.
mr president, on behalf of the s&d group, i have a small oral amendment. we need to delete the words ‘in full’ twice in this text to avoid misunderstanding, as there may be a minor technical correction of the figures. so the oral amendment is to delete the words ‘in full’ both in the middle of the amendment and at the end.
in writing. - we believe that rural development is a very important part of the eu budget. however, we are convinced that both agricultural and export aid must be reduced in favour of areas which can create growth and jobs, such as research and development programmes, erasmus and the social fund. we also welcome a reduction in funding for iter and similar energy programmes to better combat youth unemployment and to fund humanitarian aid to crisis-affected countries. we also believe that it is important to make the eu's administrative expenditure more efficient and to reduce it, which is why we welcome the budgets of the eu institutions that are below the rate of inflation. in this context, we also welcome the requirement of a single seat for the european parliament, brussels or strasbourg, which would in the long term bring considerable savings compared to today. finally, we would like to stress that the work of making further savings in the eu budget must continue.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' in the context of the directive. we have therefore voted for greater transparency in the use of european parliament funds and for reduced representation costs for the european parliament and the european commission, because it is important to clean up your own front door. we have also voted against the tobacco subsidies and the export refunds for live animals, as usual, and we agree that further budget cuts can be made to the extent that the council is now calling for. but we believe that these cuts must be made more strategically than the council obviously has the political courage to do. the council's model of using 'the cheese wheel instead of the pie crust' is in practice the same cutting in areas important for europe's development, such as investment in research and entrepreneurship, as in agricultural aid. it is not the best use of eu funds. in order to force clearer priorities on the cuts, we have therefore chosen, as a negotiating position for the final vote in december, not to vote against the european parliament's resolution this time. instead, we chose to withdraw in the end.
in writing. - we stress the importance of reducing and eventually eliminating eu subsidies to the fishing industry. the new general framework for fisheries aims to restore stocks in the long term, thereby increasing the profitability of the fisheries sector. the pressure to abolish fisheries subsidies is growing. we regret that the european parliament supported capacity-building aid for, among other things, engine replacement, temporary standstill, start-up aid and storage aid. however, as the aid for data collection and control measures is being doubled and the aid for new construction was voted out of the proposal, we decided to support the reform. however, our basic position is that taxpayers' money should not go to this kind of industrial subsidies.
in writing. − we believe that it is justified to review the eu's climate objectives for the period ahead, not least to strengthen emissions trading. sweden and the eu have a successful climate policy that has gradually reduced greenhouse gas emissions. sweden's emissions are now 20% lower than in 1990, and the eu accounts for 10% of global emissions. meanwhile, much of the world is doing nothing to reduce emissions. global emissions have increased by 50 percent since 1990. if the world is to achieve the two-degree target, it is not enough for sweden and the eu to act, other countries must take their share of responsibility. the eu's strategy for this has been a conditional 30% emissions target by 2020 if the rest of the world raises its ambitions. it hasn't worked. the eu must set higher standards for other parts of the world, not just go ahead and hope that others will follow. a first step is to require all countries to report the latest emissions figures. this should be a basic requirement in international negotiations. we note with concern the growing voices for the introduction of co2 tariffs at the eu's borders. it is the wrong way to go, and would have devastating consequences for the environment, trade, growth and development.
in writing. - we voted in favour of the final report with a view to combating organised crime, corruption and money laundering in the eu. increased cooperation at national, european and international level is essential to prevent, investigate and prosecute cross-border crime. however, we could not support all the elements of the report, such as 1) making it a crime to be part of a criminal organisation, as this does not exist in swedish law, 2) a common corporate taxation in the eu, and 3) a european prosecutor's office and its powers, which are contrary to the principle of subsidiarity. we believe that there is no 'one size fits all' solution to fight organised crime and that we must respect the different legal systems and traditions that exist in the eu. at the same time, it is important to encourage member states to increase cooperation and the exchange of good practices in the fight against cross-border crime and corruption.
in writing. - i have abstained on the vote on the iacolino report on organised crime, corruption and money laundering. it is of the utmost importance to combat organised crime effectively. the report also contains many important and positive proposals. but since the report unfortunately advocates the need to harmonize criminal law and calls for a union based on an increasingly common economic, fiscal and budgetary policy, i shall abstain. there are also other elements in the paragraphs which target terrorism, which could lead to restrictions on basic civil rights. fighting terrorism in all its forms is important, but the last decade has shown that a fight against terrorism that is not based on democratic principles is counterproductive. that is why i also choose to abstain.
in writing. - we voted in favour of the final report with a view to combating organised crime, corruption and money laundering in the eu. we believe that there is no 'one size fits all' solution to fight organised crime and that we must respect the different legal systems and traditions that exist in the eu. at the same time, it is important to encourage member states to increase cooperation and the exchange of good practices in the fight against cross-border crime and corruption.
in writing. − i voted in favour of the joint motion because, although our own group resolution calls for an immediate termination of the agreement, we support a call for suspension in a first instance to already send a strong message to the united states related to the nsa data-snooping revelations. i wish to reiterate that we have never supported the tftp agreement, due to the in-built flaws which make bulk data exchange possible, even before the nsa revelations were made.
in writing. - we support the consolidation and revision of the eu radiation protection directive. however, we see no reason to change the legal basis of the treaty on the functioning of the union, but believe that it should continue to be the euratom treaty.
in writing. − i have voted in favour of amendment 6 to the annual report from the council to the european parliament on the cfsp because the amendment stresses that international trade needs to be based on social rights and standards on health, environment and labour conditions. in addition, the amendment stresses the importance of including a broad range of stakeholders in international trade negotiations which i support. however, my support for amendment 6 does not mean that i support the eu-us ttip-negotiations. nor do i believe that ttip will bring growth and welfare to ordinary citizens – or that ttip should serve as a trade model for other regions and global actors.
the committee on budgets has been asked to deliver an opinion. - mr president! - what is it? as rapporteur for the committee on budgets, i would have liked more appropriations for infrastructure, because europe today needs productive investment more than anything else. we are glad, however, that we have been able to defend an increase, albeit an insufficient one. the committee on budgets believes that in order to get more out of the eu's efforts despite the cuts, we must use the money more efficiently. we must, among other things, provide support to those member states which do not have the expertise and administrative capacity to benefit seriously from the aid. we are also convinced that in these times of budgetary problems and banking constraints, new innovative financial instruments such as project bonds will become increasingly important. if we can get investors, such as pension funds, who are not usually keen to invest in infrastructure, to do so anyway, with guarantees from the eu budget, then this can make a crucial contribution to maintaining a reasonable level of investment despite the cuts.
the s&d group is in favour of the proposal. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. a big thank you to all those who have negotiated, not least to mr kalfin, who has had to bear the heaviest burden of this negotiation. it has been difficult because of the economic crisis but even more, i would argue, because a number of governments have only prioritized their own national interests, and this is particularly true of a number of richer net-paying countries. but budget cuts don't benefit those who really need help, because they're the net beneficiaries. the european parliament has tried to stand on two feet, respecting the economic difficulties of the member states but also taking responsibility for the future functioning of the eu. why did we then reject the council's decision in february, despite the unrelenting pressure from the member states to simply accept their decision outright? there is a crucial reason: it was a bad deal in the council. we have not succeeded in changing the level of budgetary burden but we have made a number of improvements which can have a great impact in practice. i am thinking above all of flexibility. the current outdated rules have meant that a large part of the budget has never been used, but we are changing that now. i am thinking of political priorities; we have moved resources forward for youth unemployment, for research and so on. in addition, we have acquired an opportunity to renegotiate mid-term. this makes for a very good deal. i'm not going to love it, but it's so good that i recommend a yes vote.
blue-card question. – ms helga trüpel is talking about missed chances. the question is not what the member states could have done – i agree they should have done much more – but whether parliament in this situation can accomplish all the things ms trüpel is asking for by voting ‘no’? my answer is no, it cannot. i am an old trade unionist and i know that there is no way you can win a fight over wages in an economic crisis. it is the same thing here: to throw europe into a budget crisis in this situation would not improve matters. let us come back through the mid-term review; today we would only make things worse by creating a budget crisis – that is for sure.
in writing. - i voted against the budgetary framework. it is good that we are freezing expenditure and showing greater budgetary responsibility and socioeconomic thinking. i fully support this approach. but europe is in the throes of the deepest economic and social crisis since the end of the second world war. mass unemployment, poverty and growing social problems have put several eu countries in a humanitarian crisis that particularly affects women. this development also threatens democracy. neither the multiannual budgetary plan nor the eu's current market-liberal policy presents a way out of the crisis. as usual, gender equality and aid are being undermined while the budget for military projects is increasing. climate change is also being ignored with large investments in highways and fossil energy. the large agricultural policy item is also directly unfriendly to the environment. the large subsidies, which go mainly to large-scale industrialised farms, are also leading to the exclusion of both european small farms and farms in the south where subsidised products can be sold at dumped prices. these are the main reasons why i voted against the financial framework.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' in the definition of the term 'competent authority'. but i cannot support a budget that still has all the bad parts intact. a large part of the structural funds supports the construction of motorways, and the eu still invests large sums in projects with inoperative fusion power rather than putting the money into developing renewable energy sources. the agricultural policy still contains far too much direct aid, and in addition the new agricultural reform will allow money to be moved (15-25%) from the rural programme to the direct aid programme, which gives even more money to direct aid. there is also the possibility of export subsidies for agricultural products, a measure that hits hard at poor developing countries and which the eu has promised to end in 2013. a promise that has not been kept! in an economic crisis, the eu could have reallocated scarce resources to invest in the future, research, energy efficiency and investments in and for our young people. unfortunately, this was a missed opportunity.

in writing. - i've lowered my voice. the main cause of the widespread crisis, mass unemployment and poverty in the eu is the liberal market policy, in which the interests of banking and finance capital are at the centre. ordinary people, especially women, are being hit hard by the crisis, which has been deepened by the functioning of emu, where the single currency does not, among other things, allow the countries in crisis to devalue. more money for the social fund will in no way solve the deep crisis. change requires a new policy that puts human rights, employment and welfare at the centre. the european union must also be able to take action to combat the spread of the disease.
in writing. - i voted for the report. i am doing so in order to provide the workers in this case with financial support for retraining and/or other measures to find new jobs. however, i would like to emphasize that the main problem with regard to the high level of unemployment in the eu is the austerity policies advocated by the european commission and decided by the governments of the member states, which hit women particularly hard. to create new jobs in europe, a new economic policy with massive public and environmentally friendly investments is needed. investments for the benefit of society are needed in education, health and care, but also in infrastructure, railways, public transport, energy-efficient housing and sustainable energy systems.
in writing. - parliament voted in november on the mobilisation of the european globalisation adjustment fund. since 2009, i have voted against all proposals to use the fund as a rule. this is because i do not think that the fund is the right instrument to help workers affected by structural change. the fund has grown, evolved and received its own allocation. it will therefore exist for the foreseeable future. so it is a matter of making the best of the situation. to benefit from the fund, applicants must meet very strict requirements for co-financing and long-term employment measures. that is why i voted in favour of the report. it does not change my view that the creation of the fund was more than debatable and that it should be dismantled. europe and the eu can respond to competition and structural changes in the market in much better, more efficient and more sustainable ways in the long term. but now the situation is such that the fund exists, and it is on this basis that we must act.
in writing. - i abstained in the final vote on the erasmus programme. the left party is extremely positive about exchange programmes which, among other things, have led to and are leading to important cross-border understanding and knowledge. unfortunately, we see that there are problems with lumping all the programmes together under one umbrella and also that the system now tends to acquire a certain unnecessary supranationality.
in writing. - we voted today in favour of the report on the creation of 'erasmus for all', the union's programme for education, training, youth and sport. however, we do not support the creation of a european loan guarantee for erasmus master students, as we believe that this is a national competence and not something that the eu should be concerned with or allocate funds to.
in writing. i chose to abstain in the final vote. although the creative europe programme contains many important issues of cultural diversity and cultural development, i see that there is also a federalist approach and unnecessary detailed management. i also think that too much is being invested in the market adaptation of culture and that it is unfortunate that it is linked to horizon 2020. a free and growing culture is more than the number of jobs, and i chose to abstain.
in writing. - i voted against the report. the aim of this programme is to 'strengthen remembrance and increase citizens' involvement at union level'. for me, this is another attempt by the eu to try to impose a sense of union citizenship through propaganda. but feelings of belonging across borders cannot be forced, and certainly not by propaganda/educational programmes. real cohesion is built in people's encounters with each other, through joint work and mutual solidarity across borders.
in writing. - the ten-t report is complex. this is a huge infrastructure project, covering all modes of transport, which aims to link the eu. it has been a directive which has now become a regulation, giving the eu the power to govern in detail. on the other hand, there is a strong focus on extending and connecting railways within the eu. that is why i chose not to vote on the report.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of radioactive waste, which is a key element in the protection of the environment. if the report had been linked to a settlement, i could have supported the proposal.
mr president, i would like to thank you. as a former mayor of northern sweden, after two years in the european parliament, i can say that the local and regional level has very good friends in the european parliament, as well as in the commission, with commissioner johannes hahn at the head. what is needed to achieve the eu 2020 targets is a lot of committed people from civil society, business, municipalities and regions working locally to create the growth that this tool actually gives us. i think we also have to look at two other things that are very good about the decision we make tomorrow. the first is that we have created the conditions for simplification and efficiency in work and the second is the principle of partnership. on these two issues - that we are simplifying and that the member states must have a dialogue with the local and regional level and other stakeholders - we must, together, the european parliament and the commission, keep a close eye on the member states to ensure that they do the right thing on these two issues.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i think the most important progress made in these negotiations is that the structural funds will now seriously become investment funds. the swedish eu minister once called cohesion policy the policy of the 1950s. she was probably thinking of the time when old mining and shipyard regions were given support. it was wrong then, but it's gonna be even more wrong in the future. because now we are supposed to use the funds as instruments to implement eu policies on innovation, infrastructure, climate action, energy efficiency. it is not the policy of the 1950s, but rather the instrument of the policy of the future. in many regions, eu investment is crucial for jobs and growth, especially now that the crisis has left banks unable to lend and public budgets are being cut. the eu's investment is crucial, and that is what the funds are intended to do in the future.
the committee on economic and monetary affairs has tabled an opinion. - gender equality is basically about giving women and men equal opportunities to influence society. it's about power. in the committee on economic and monetary affairs we stated that the eu must take bold legislative action to achieve this objective. the committee therefore shares the commission's view. shared responsibility in company boardrooms leads to better decisions. the right to property is also an important principle that must be respected, and here is a problem. companies in the financial sector have a higher proportion of women on their boards compared to other sectors. however, one third of the boards of directors in this sector have no female board members. i am pleased to have received support for my proposal to extend the scope of directives to the eu and its institutions, including the ecb the european central bank. the eu institutions must set a good example; we cannot ask others to do what we cannot do ourselves. finally, we need men, that is, me and other men, to be at home more with our children, and more women must be given more power in the boards of publicly traded companies.
mr president, i would like to thank you. ladies and gentlemen, let me give you a picture of what the boardrooms of europe look like today. in the largest listed companies in the eu, 17% of board members are women. if we talk about the chairmen of the board, they're 96% male. in sweden, there are more men named johan than women in the ceo positions of listed companies. the development is too slow and the thick glass ceiling has consistently kept women out of the top jobs. this is a waste of not only expertise but also money. studies show that companies with the highest proportion of women on their boards have better financial performance than companies without female board representation. opponents of the directive say we must protect private property rights, but important parts of company law already rest on eu directives. in addition, the largest companies in the eu operate across borders in the single market. it is therefore both reasonable and logical that these companies have the same rules of the game. at the vote tomorrow, we have the opportunity to make the first crack in the glass ceiling. together, we can break it. (applause) i would like to thank you.
mr president, i would like to thank you. yes, tomorrow we will probably pass this proposal on gender quotas in company boards. we will do so on the basis of a proposal from a committee of 19 men and 9 women, and here in the european parliament we are 70% men and 30% women. the european central bank, where we actually had some influence, seems to be the closest thing to its saudi counterpart in terms of representation of women, because not a single woman sits on its board. we are not going to vote for the report, but we are going to vote for the report, and we are going to vote for the report. this is nothing but a great hypocrisy. i assume that all members, especially those in the s&d and epp who are going to vote for this proposal tomorrow, will likewise be in favour of quotas for lists to the european parliament as well as to their own party boards at home in their member states and other bodies where parties appoint officers. it's gonna be very exciting to watch. or is it that it's much easier to demand equality from others than to fix it at home? there's a lot of cleaning up to do in your own backyard. i would stress that the proposal rests on an extremely shaky and fragile legal basis. its european added value is virtually non-existent. this is the prototype of a matter where each member state should legislate on its own because our countries' cultures are very different. in the process, i have worked with pragmatically minded colleagues such as olle schmidt at econ to try to bring about a reasonable regulatory framework such as the danish comply or explain legislation. unfortunately, the majority have chosen something else. i'm sorry about that. mr president, i have noted with increasing surprise that the conservative and social democratic groups have shown no interest in having a constructive dialogue on this. now it's going to be quotas, and i'm sorry. men should be where the children and the old are, and women where the money and the power and the legislation are. that is not what this proposal will achieve.
madam president, i would like to thank you. commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. as for the whole package, there is one small detail that i think will have a huge impact on the future and it is perhaps the smallest detail that is the most controversial. it's the organic tree. in fact, it doesn't matter if we have three, five, seven or 10 percent, what matters is that we create corridors in the farming landscape where the ecological system services can flourish. the second thing i am very happy about is the concept of 'active farmer', although it has been rather washed out. secondly, i would like to thank my colleagues who have worked on the rural development regulation. i think we've done a very good job. i think pillar 2 of the rural programme is very good; it reflects the endless cultural and biological diversity that the european countryside faces. the conditions are so varied, of course: in that programme there is freedom to make adjustments to local national conditions, but there is also a little danger that it could lead to the re-nationalisation of large parts of agricultural policy. finally, i would like to say that i am disappointed with the funding. i am disappointed that so much continues to go to direct direct aid and so little to payment for environmental services. my personal dream is to have an agricultural policy where farmers are paid for their environmental services and where it is even profitable to stand up for our collective interests.

in writing. - i have voted on all the reports. all these funds are part of the eu's strategy to increase the centralisation of power to the union and to increase its integration. a federalist plan that i'm opposed to. the reports also show that the funds are guided by the essentially neoliberal europe 2020 strategy. a political line i oppose. i do not deny that there are a number of positive projects that have been launched with the support of these funds, but it is also true that the most widespread fraud and waste within the eu is done with funds from the funds. in the current economic crisis, it is claimed that the funds could help bring about change. it is an illusion. real change requires a new policy that puts human rights, employment and welfare at the centre. the european union must also be able to take action to combat the spread of the disease.

madam president, i would like to thank you. we have to do more for equality, that's right, that's true. but the way forward is not legislation on quotas for women in publicly traded companies, but we will decide that at home. i have long been committed to having more women in senior positions and more female entrepreneurs. at eu level, we should keep it on the agenda, but not at the top. we're gonna put pressure on the business community, but we're not gonna jeopardize free enterprise. the way forward must be to build corporate responsibility to promote women in leadership positions. this applies to all parts of business, not just stock companies, and outside of business, not just boards. madam president, quotas at eu level will not solve the problems of gender equality. in sweden, that would mean just under 250 women, and in malta, no women at all. we must make it easier for women entrepreneurs, for example by opening up the internal market to a number of sectors, especially services in the health sector.
in writing. - i am a strong advocate of gender quotas and believe that this is an effective way to combat stereotypes, increase democracy and break the informal male quota system. i voted against the report, however, partly because the resolution addresses gender quotas on company boards as if it were the most important issue of equality, and partly because it wants eu legislation in this area. the left party is opposed to the transfer of more power to the eu and believes that the eu's neoliberal policies and what they have done for women require completely different priorities, such as a new socio-economic policy that makes the gender power structure visible in society and invests in jobs and welfare for all.
in writing. - when the european parliament voted on gender quotas in company boards, i chose to vote against. improving gender equality in sweden and the rest of europe is of paramount importance, but quotas are not the way to go. instead, i believe it is about stimulating female entrepreneurship and increasing the possibility of combining work and family life. i also believe that this is a matter of subsidiarity; it is in the individual member states that we should decide on legislation for swedish companies, not at eu level.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'common carrier' in the definition of the term 'commercial vehicle'. agricultural aid can only be justified if it generates added value for society at large and is directed towards key actions that the market cannot cope with. eu agricultural policy must deliver greater benefits for the environment and society at large. we would therefore like to see more money diverted from direct aid to active measures that benefit the environment and develop rural areas. the agricultural policy part dealing with rural development is the only one of the proposals that we support. we are very critical of other aspects of policy. as regards the common organization of the market in agricultural products, we would like to see a continuation of market adjustment. the regulation of european food production has a clear negative impact on the development and competitiveness of the sector. we therefore wanted to see the various protection systems and production subsidies that exist today abolished. on the one hand, they hinder the improvement of production efficiency and on the other hand, they create unfair conditions for food producers in different member states. we also want to highlight the decision not to completely abolish eu export subsidies as a major setback in the development towards a policy which is also solidarity with the world around us. we social democrats are aware that sweden and europe need an agricultural policy which is modern and green. the commission has already proposed a number of measures to improve the quality of the environment. we therefore believe that the eu's agricultural policy will continue to be an area in need of major reform as eu cooperation moves into the next mandate. we will actively work towards that.
in writing. - i voted against all the reports. the eu's agricultural policy is probably the most expensive and dysfunctional policy in the union. it is clear that big business and a wealthy agricultural lobby have had the most influence over the seven-year plan. a report by the oecd found that eu agricultural policies had 'negative effects on rural employment'. a group of public finance experts recently reviewed the eu-supported swedish rural development programme. they stated that the annual aid of sek 5 billion has 'obscure effects and doubtful benefits to society'. the seven-year plan calls for investments in 'green solutions'. after studying the plan, the swedish nature conservation society correctly concluded that 'the alleged greening is a bad joke'. export subsidies for agricultural products sold to developing countries will continue. for the poorest farmers in the world, this means continued disaster. for example, so far tens of thousands of west african chicken farmers have lost their jobs. they cannot compete with the eu's export-supported cheap chicken pieces. the seven-year plan is an obstacle to anyone who wants a modern agricultural policy. such policies address vital issues such as the environment, climate, rural development, organic farming, improved animal welfare and solidarity with the global south. in the long term, the only right thing to do is to renationalize agricultural policy. for these reasons i am voting against the proposed common agricultural policy.

in writing. - we chose to vote against the agreement on direct payments for agricultural policy because we are very critical of the fact that the budget for direct payments is unchanged, while rural development aid, including the important environmental aid, is being reduced. we are also very critical of the fact that as much as 15% of the financial resources can be moved between pillars 2 and 1 of the cap through so-called flexibility between the pillars. in some of the member states, where direct payments are below the eu average, it will be possible to transfer as much as a quarter of rural development funding to direct payments. this is something we are very concerned about. we also object to the fact that the financial components of the cap reform were re-prioritized and decided by the eu heads of state or government behind closed doors, which meant that these components were not negotiable. this is totally unacceptable and, above all, undemocratic.

in writing. - i voted for the report. this is to extend the increased co-financing rate for the structural and cohesion funds to the member states and regions most affected by the crisis and to extend the deadline for recovery to the end of the current mff. this is to simplify management and speed up investment. but this measure does not solve the deep crisis; change requires a new policy that puts human rights, employment and welfare at the centre. the european union must also be able to take action to combat the spread of the disease.
madam president, today, for the first time, parliament has voted on its right to decide when and where to meet. we have made an historic breakthrough and have paved the way for treaty change. i am an active member of the single seat steering group because i am committed to a more effective parliament. european citizens are asking for this. they do not understand why, or accept that, we waste eur 150 million and 19 000 tonnes of co2 every year on the travelling circus between brussels and strasbourg. the majority of this parliament has already voted in favour of savings by means of a single seat. let me emphasise that this is not a vote against france. this is not a vote against strasbourg. this is a vote in favour of a more cost-efficient parliament which decides its own calendar. we stand ready to pursue a constructive dialogue with all those who want to find viable solutions for all european institution seats. however, the deadlock must be broken and now it is time for the member states to act and take shared responsibility.
for the alde group. president. - the debate is closed. i would like to thank the commission, the other rapporteurs and the shadow rapporteurs and the council for the very good cooperation, as my colleagues have said in this context. from the beginning, we had a good proposal from the commission as a starting point, and thanks to a constructive dialogue, we have together developed a research and innovation programme of which we can be proud and which has the potential to strengthen europe's competitiveness through promoted research and innovation. as has been said before, we are at a time when investment in research and innovation is really needed, and those who invest in research and innovation also know that it will make them better equipped to face the future. i am particularly pleased with the clear markings that exist, such as the focus on excellence and the possibility of linking our efforts in the research programme with the structural funds. i am also pleased with the clear focus on innovation and the link between research and innovation. this is where we can see the chain of innovation from idea to market. this means that actors from academia, research institutions and industry can and need to work together more closely throughout the innovation chain. the open approach to the world that software represents and the open, strong open access policy are key ingredients in the present proposal. an important component is the so-called 'kicks' - the knowledge and innovation clusters - which play an important role in bringing together educational institutions, research centres and companies to develop and exploit the entrepreneurs of tomorrow. i am also glad that we have a clear focus on green transition and green energy and that we are also highlighting the gender aspect of the programme. we have so far under-exploited the potential and resources of women in research and innovation. i am particularly pleased, as a representative of the alde group, with the efforts being made in favour of smes, and i am also pleased with the strong support from the council on this issue. i know from my own experience that it can be difficult to change structures and so on, so i also wanted to make a comment to the commission for the future work, namely how important it is that the new unit can also work on really pursuing these possibilities for new structures. i would also like to conclude by saying that the kick-offs and the sme instrument offer an opportunity to widen the scope for more researchers, more countries and more member states to participate in the work. i also see it as an opportunity to realise the ambitions of increased and widespread participation, in particular through the kicks and the interaction with the structural funds.
madam president, i would like to thank you. when malala stood here a few hours ago and thanked for the sakharov prize, she said, among other things, that "everything starts with a book and a pen". translated into european terms, it should all start with research and innovation, because that is our book and pen. research and development is simply one of the most sensible and important things the eu does. i am therefore pleased with the horizon programme, i am pleased with the kick-offs, i am pleased with the renewable energy and energy efficiency programme which i very much hope will be implemented. i am pleased that the ageing european generation can claim the public interest over the market interest in this future area. i am also very pleased that my own proposal to include forestry policy is included and that the socialist rapporteur, teresa riera madurell, has pushed for the demands of excellence and, not least, has brought the gender dimension into both research and the research community. in conclusion, i think we should hold on to the taxpayers' money. like judith a merkies, however, i must regret that we have not been able to agree clearly on redistributing more from agricultural policy to research and development. it would have been to invest fully in the future instead of bending over backwards and trying to raise new money. redistribution is possible!
madam president, i would like to thank you. i too would like to thank both rapporteurs for a well-written and balanced report. i have tried to push this report in a direction which safeguards subsidiarity. countries like sweden have a long tradition of neutrality, and i would remind you that we outside nato have a different view on many issues. for countries like sweden, the focus is on the emerging common security policy rather than on the coordination of the countries' defence industries or the coordination of defence processes. solidarity, concern and the feeling that your security is our security must be the values around which we unite and which we have as a basis for working together for security. nevertheless, i believe that there is a benefit in limiting the eu's involvement to security guarantees and that the coordination of defence processes and equipment takes place within the framework of nato. if the development of processes and materials takes place within such a structure, we will have a better security policy at a lower cost because we can then integrate within the whole of nato instead of having one structure for the eu and another for the others.
mr president, i would like to thank you. at the moment some of our colleagues are in warsaw, but we know that expectations of that meeting are not very high. at the same time, the number of storms continues to increase, biodiversity is declining, and the oceans are becoming over-fertile. life, on the other hand, has shown success in its 20 years of existence. natura 2000 is a good example. life is a programme that seeks to bring about change at home and to contribute to increased environmental awareness. but an ambitious program needs resources. it is good that life has received more funds, i agree, but within the framework of the eu budget, life has far too few resources. if life is also to be integrated into existing funds, i see a risk that this program could be de-prioritized, but i hope i'm wrong. the eu must continue to take more responsibility for protecting biodiversity and reducing global warming.
in writing. - i have voted against these three reports. the basic line of horizon 2020 is closely linked to the europe 2020 strategy and is therefore also strongly characterised by a neoliberal mindset that puts the interests of business at the forefront. this limits the possibilities for independent and free research. it is clear that many of the corporate lobbyists had a major influence on the design. there are of course also positive elements in this package of reports, but as the main line is so neoliberal, i choose to vote no.
in writing. - life is an environment programme with a great deal of responsibility, which seeks to transform the eu into an ecologically sustainable society. with great responsibility and much to do, a budget commensurate with the responsibility is required. the gue and the environmental movement demanded 1% of the eu budget but received only a fraction of the sum. however, i chose to vote in favour of the proposal because life is a necessary and functioning eu programme to achieve sustainable development.
in writing. - i voted against the two reports by mr gahler and mr koppa. these reports are intended to increase the militarisation of the eu and also to provide support for the arms industry. it also blurs the lines between civil and military research and proposes that the social funds be used for these purposes. this is a path the eu must not take. i am also very concerned about the proposals for a permanent defence council and a more influential eda funded by the eu budget. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environment from harmful substances. a lot of the language feels like it's taken from orwell's 1984. for example, the csdp is a basic pillar of the european integration process. to me, that sounds like "war is peace". i hope that the council of ministers meeting in december will not go in the direction that parliament wants to take in these reports.
